Russell Brand was a 'sexual predator', prosecution alleges as rape trial begins

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 17:55:53 GMT

Russell Brand was described as "ruthless" and a "sexual predator" by the prosecution at the start of his trial for rape and sexual assault on Wednesday.

The 51-year-old actor and comedian faces three counts of rape, three charges of sexual assault and one of indecent assault.

The allegations, made by six female complainants, span a 10-year period between 1999 and 2009. He denies the charges.

Riel Karmy-Jones KC, for the prosecution, told Southwark Crown Court in London the women "did not know each other", "have never met each other" and "came forward independently of each other".

The barrister said: "The circumstances of the offending were as wide and as varied as the defendant's career."

She said that "regardless of who the women were and what they said or did, Brand was not deterred".

"His desire, strong desire, for some form of sexual contact was indiscriminate and voracious," she told the court.

The prosecutor told jurors that Brand would be "nice when he wanted to be, funny - undoubtedly - charming, likeable even, when he wanted something in return".

But she said he would also be "demanding, entitled, a flirt with a huge sexual appetite - childlike in his expectation that his every desire should be met".

The prosecutor said Brand would "appear indifferent, normal and untroubled by what had just occurred" after the encounters.

She added that "sometimes he would leave promptly after the incident, sometimes making an excuse to get away, or sometimes simply continuing as though nothing wrong had happened".

On Wednesday, the jury heard details of the alleged offences.

The first complainant says she met Brand before he was famous in 1999, the jury was told.

She had attended the Labour Party conference and went to a fringe event where she spoke to Brand. He later allegedly raped her.

The prosecution said when Brand was questioned by police about this allegation of rape, "he said if it happened, he didn't remember the incident".

He added that he did not remember the first complainant's name and that he "had never engaged in non-consensual activity".

The second complainant worked for MTV at the time when she says Brand indecently assaulted her.

She said Brand asked her where the toilets were and the woman offered to show him.

When they reached the toilets, prosecutors say, Brand asked the woman to "come in" before grabbing her forearm.

She resisted by struggling and pulling back. He had what she described as a desperate, wild-eyed look on his face, as he said: "Please please come in – oh go on."

The third complainant says Brand orally raped her in a toilet after meeting her at a party in 2004.

Within a minute of meeting, in the middle of a conversation, he lurched forward and grabbed her breasts and squeezed them with both hands, the jury was told.

The prosecution said the woman remembered he had a "big, weird grin" on his face as he did so, then grabbed her arm and pulled her towards the toilets.

The same woman said that as they were driving to another venue, Brand exposed himself, the court heard.

"He said, 'Come on, come on, suck it' – to which she just said, 'No no'," the prosecution added. "He simply laughed but appeared to accept her refusal."

The fourth complainant says Brand pushed her up against a wall at Capital Radio in 2004 or 2005 and kissed her "sloppily and aggressively", and "grabbed her body, at her breasts, sides and buttocks, aggressively".

She was able to push him away and he "just sniggered, like it was a joke", the prosecutor said.

The jury then heard how the fifth complainant says she met Brand when he was performing at the Hammersmith Apollo in London in 2009.

The prosecution said their encounter began with consensual activity, but that he raped her anally on a later occasion.

The jury heard how the woman "spent months and years blaming herself", becoming anxious and unwell.

They were told Brand texted the woman after seeing her at an event the following summer, saying: "I wanna push you further."

She is said to have replied: "I think you pushed me far enough – you treated me like an animal." He allegedly replied: "I'm sorry."

The sixth complainant - who has accused Brand of sexual assault - says he demanded a massage when she was on a work assignment in 2009.

He "fell to his knees and placed his head directly into her groin area", the prosecution said.

After the accounts of the final two women were heard, the prosecutor said Brand told police in 2023 "he has never engaged in non-consensual sexual activity, and that any accounts had been reinvented or imagined after a defamatory documentary and subsequent press reporting".

On Wednesday afternoon, Brand's former flatmate Mark Pinheiro was the first witness to give evidence.

Pinheiro told jurors that Brand had told him he may have gone a "bit too far" with a woman when visiting the Labour Party conference in 1999.

"He said that things got a little bit heated and a little bit out of hand, and he thinks that maybe he did go a little bit too far.

"Those were his words - 'I think I may have gone a bit too far'."

Pinheiro was pressed under cross-examination about whether he accurately remembered the comment, but he told the court he was sure Brand had said "things may have gone a little bit too far" or "I may have taken things a little too far".

The trial continues and is expected to last for six weeks.

Brand was accompanied by his wife Laura to the court on Wednesday.

He rose to fame as a stand-up comedian and became a household name as host of TV shows such as Big Brother's Big Mouth, and with his own radio programmes on stations including BBC Radio 2 and 6 Music.

He went on to establish a Hollywood career, starring in films including Forgetting Sarah Marshall and Get Him To The Greek. In recent years, he has turned to podcasting and now lives in Florida in the United States.

Drop-off charges at UK airports to be reviewed after sharp increase

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 17:46:30 GMT

Airport drop-off charges are to be reviewed by the government following sharp price increases in recent years.

Prices at major UK airports have risen by a third since the summer of 2025 on average, according to one analysis, with Gatwick and Stansted drop-off charges found to be the most expensive, jumping from £7 to £10.

Such charges at Edinburgh Airport have risen from £6 to £8.50, while fees at Bournemouth increased from £5 to £8, the same price as Leeds Bradford and London City.

The Department for Transport confirmed it had committed to a review, saying it was "vital to ensure travel remains accessible and that passengers are treated fairly".

The government added it hoped airports would "exercise restraint on rises, particularly where there are limited public transport alternatives".

The RAC, which conducted the analysis in July this year, has campaigned against increases in drop-off charges. It said a review of "kiss and fly" fees would be "music to the ears to drivers".

"We know airport drop-off charges are a growing source of anger and frustration," said Rod Dennis, senior policy officer at the RAC.

"Four-in-five drivers we've surveyed believe they are a 'rip-off', with just one-in-10 understanding why airports charge what they do."

The RAC's research found London City Airport, which only introduced charges in 2026, was the most expensive on a per-minute basis.

Its £8 charge for five minutes works out at £1.60 per minute. Belfast International Airport now charges £5 for 10 minutes in the drop-off zone, up £2 on last year.

None of the top 10 airports in the European Union charge for drop-offs, according to the RAC.

It argues that international airports should not charge for dropping off passengers, however, the industry said the charges were a neccesary part of the business model of airports.

Supporters of such charges also say they encourage travellers to use of more environmentally friendly modes of transport.

"Airports outside the UK are typically publicly owned and in receipt of state subsidies and tax exemptions, while UK airports are generally privately owned and subject to the full array of commercial rates of taxation," said Karen Dee, chief executive of Airports UK, which represents airports.

She added airports offered a "range of options for those travelling to terminals, including free drop off points" and that "any review" by the government would find airports make "excellent use of a variety of ways to manage traffic and air pollution".

The Aviation Environment Federation campaign group has previously defended the fees, arguing they help reduce congestion and air pollution around airports.

Earlier this month, the government announced extra charges for parents sitting with their children when flying would be scrapped in time for next year's summer holidays.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham said the changes were part of a series of "everyday fixes" to ease cost of living pressures on households.

Kenya confirms second Ebola case - a relative of the first victim

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 17:23:01 GMT

Kenya has confirmed a second case of Ebola, 10 days after a businessman died of the virus after he had travelled to the East African nation from the Democratic Republic of Congo.

The new patient, a 39-year-old man, is a close relative of the first case and was already in quarantine when he fell ill on Tuesday, Kenya's health ministry says.

The incubation period for Ebola ranges from two to 21 days, meaning those exposed to the virus may not become ill for several weeks.

Detecting his illness early had improved his chances of recovery and limited the chance of him passing the virus on to others, the ministry said, as it urged the public to "stay calm but vigilant".

"Both cases are linked, and there is no evidence of spread in the wider community," the ministry said.

"Kenya remains open, with no restrictions on travel, trade or public gatherings", it added.

The 40-year-old Kenyan man who died on 5 October was diagnosed with Ebola shortly after being admitted to hospital in the capital, Nairobi.

A relative had taken him there shortly after picking him up at the airport. He had travelled from DR Congo, through Uganda to Kenya without his illness being detected.

Since his death, officials had determined that he had been in contact with 128 people - and since Tuesday 113 of them had been found, the health ministry said.

Of these, 108 were in quarantine in Kenya and tracing of the remaining 15 continued, it said.

More than 4,000 people have died from Ebola in DR Congo this year, in what is the second most deadly known outbreak to date.

The Ebola virus spreads through contact with infected bodily fluids such as blood or vomit.

"Anyone with fever, unusual tiredness, muscle or joint pains, vomiting, diarrhoea or unexplained bleeding should seek care promptly at the nearest health facility," Kenya's health ministry said.

The current outbreak in DR Congo, which began in May, has been caused by the Bundibugyo species of Ebola, which is relatively rare, with no approved vaccine or treatment - though trials are under way.

Shortly after DR Congo declared an outbreak of Ebola in May, it was also reported in Uganda, where two people died.

However, the World Health Organization (WHO) declared that county free of the disease in August.

The Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention, the continent's leading public health agency, has reconstructed the Kenyan patient's movements for the six weeks leading up to his death.

It revealed that he first started feeling unwell in DR Congo on 15 September - and showed how several opportunities to detect the infection were missed, including during several visits to Congolese medical facilities.

He then managed to travel through Uganda to Kenya - and was again not detected at airports screenings.

Health experts say it highlights the limitations of relying on temperature checks and self-declarations.

I do not have confidence UK is safe from Russian threats - Swinney

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 16:57:21 GMT

First Minister John Swinney says he does not have confidence the UK is safe from potential Russian threats.

The SNP leader said the UK government, which is in charge of Britain's defences, did not have a sufficient "presence" in vulnerable areas off Scotland's coast.

He said he was "very worried" by security fears highlighted this week by the head of MI5, Sir Ken McCallum, who named Russia as "aggressor number one".

Speaking to BBC Scotland News ahead of the SNP's annual conference, Swinney also denied that independence would weaken Scotland's security.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham has said his government is committed to investing in defence, including nuclear deterrence, while not seeking to escalate tensions with Russia.

Asked about the MI5 chief's comments, the first minister said he was "very worried" about a "lack of coastal and offshore protection".

He identified waters north of Shetland as an area of significant Russian activity.

"I don't think we've got nearly the presence in those areas that we require today," he told BBC Scotland News.

"There's an obligation on the United Kingdom government to make sure that the country is safe. And I don't think the defence posture of the United Kingdom enables me to have that confidence."

Scotland is home to the UK's nuclear submarine fleet at Faslane, west of Glasgow. The SNP wants those weapons to be removed, but is in favour of an independent Scotland joining Nato.

Swinney insisted that independence would not hamper Scotland's security, but rather give the country "more choices".

The SNP leader said he had "always been a believer in defence co-operation between Scotland and other countries", citing Canada joining the EU's SAFE defence programme.

Responding to the first minister's comments, a Ministry of Defence Spokesperson said:

"The first duty of government is to keep its citizens safe, and this government is investing £298bn over the next four years to ensure we can tackle rising threats.

"The Navy is helping to patrol Nato's northern flank, with this year's carrier strike group deploying to the Arctic and the high north.

"To tackle these threats, we're also investing in new frigates for the Navy and high-tech uncrewed vessels as part of our Hybrid Navy, backed by £1.5bn in funding."

Delivering a speech at the UN General Assembly last month, Burnham said his government would invest in defence and build up the UK's "hard power and deterrence".

He added: "But we will not play Russia's game of trading escalatory rhetoric. Unlike Russia, we do not seek confrontation."

SNP members are gathering in Aberdeen for the party's annual conference, which starts on Thursday and ends with Swinney's keynote speech on Saturday afternoon.

Burnham has also selected this weekend to visit Scotland. He is expected to travel to the central belt to discuss plans to devolve power to local communities.

Swinney, who has invited the Labour leader to the SNP conference, said he had "not yet" accepted the offer.

The first minister welcomed Burnham's recent announcement that he wanted to secure better trade terms with the EU for producers of salmon, shellfish, and seed potatoes.

The prime minister also wants to discuss even closer ties with the bloc and has not ruled out a future referendum on rejoining.

Swinney told the BBC: "I think it's an interesting precedent that if we can reopen an issue that's been decided by a referendum, then we can also reopen Scottish independence."

Ahead of the conference, the SNP announced it would launch a new independence campaign - including a national day of action, digital and broadcast adverts, and an independence survey delivered to almost 1.5m people.

The Scottish government is aiming to hold a second referendum by 2031.

The Supreme Court has ruled that could only happen with Westminster's consent.

Arguing the case for a second vote, Swinney points to the record number of pro-independence MSPs elected to Holyrood in May - though the proportion of pro-independence votes decreased from about 50% to about 41% since 2021.

Burnham said in the Commons last month that a second referendum could be held once there was a clear consensus and public opinion had changed.

However, he later insisted that there was no consensus, adding that such a vote remained "off limits".

Burnham later made a special mention of Swinney In his speech to the Labour Party conference, insisting he was "utterly determined" to prove wrong the SNP leader's prediction that he would be the last prime minister of the UK.

Swinney was also challenged on the preparedness of the NHS for winter pressures after an emergency doctor warned of "unsafe levels of care" and a "toxic normalisation" of corridor care.

Dr Fiona Hunter, vice-chair of the Royal College of Emergency Medicine in Scotland, told BBC Scotland News that she was "completely terrified" of the situation.

The first minister said he could "hear, understand and appreciate" these concerns and accepted that there were "significant challenges" in the system.

However, he said hospital flow plans were designed to reduce pressures and the Scottish government intended to end corridor care "at the earliest possible opportunity".

Sally Rooney books withdrawn in UK over Palestine Action support

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 16:56:21 GMT

Normal People author Sally Rooney has said her novels will no longer be supplied to booksellers in the UK as a result of her support for Palestine Action.

The Irish novelist said last year she intended to carry on using her royalties to support the group, which is a proscribed terrorist organisation in the UK.

In a statement to BBC News, Rooney said further publication of her work could constitute a "serious criminal offence", so she had agreed with her publisher Faber not to continue supplying her books in the UK.

"I have made every effort to be honest with the public about my beliefs and intentions, and I am wholly prepared to accept the consequences," she said.

Ebook editions have already been removed from sale in the UK, although many book shops still have existing stock of physical copies.

Waterstones confirmed to the BBC that Faber had advised there were no restrictions on booksellers keeping their current copies on the shelves.

Rooney has sold more than six million books globally, including 2.3 million in the UK, according to NielsenIQ BookData.

Normal People was released in 2018 and turned into a hit TV series two years later, effectively launching the acting careers of Paul Mescal and Daisy Edgar-Jones.

Rooney's other novels are Intermezzo, Conversations With Friends and Beautiful World, Where Are You.

Publisher Faber told BBC News: "Current legislation in the UK has led to circumstances where Faber cannot pass on UK earnings to Sally Rooney for her work.

"To sell a writer's work and not be able to pass on earnings is an unconscionable situation for a publisher.

"As such, and in full agreement with Sally, we have taken the decision that we can no longer supply her works in the UK in any format."

Faber added: "We remain the proud publisher of her remarkable body of work, which we will continue to sell in other territories."

The UK government proscribed Palestine Action as a terrorist organisation in 2025, saying it had a "long history" of criminal damage.

The group's activities have included targeting arms companies linked to Israel's war in Gaza.

In her statement on Wednesday, Rooney said: "In July 2025, the direct-action protest group Palestine Action was proscribed as a terrorist organisation in the UK.

"The following month, I wrote an opinion piece expressing my intention to go on supporting Palestine Action, which is not a proscribed or otherwise prohibited group here in the Republic of Ireland."

In her article last August in the Irish Times, Rooney said she would continue using proceeds from her work to support the group despite that being an offence in the UK, and cancelled a UK trip to pick up an award over fears she would be arrested.

On Wednesday, Rooney continued: "As a result, the fulfilment of contracts to publish my work in the UK could now constitute a serious criminal offence.

"After a process of consultation and deliberation, and with my full agreement, my publisher Faber has therefore determined that it can no longer supply my work within the UK."

Palestine Action co-founder Huda Ammori said Rooney had taken "a brave and principled stand from the moment plans to ban Palestine Action were announced".

Ammori said the author's work being withdrawn marked "a new low in the escalating assault on free speech caused by the proscription of Palestine Action".

More than 3,700 people are believed to have been arrested in the UK over alleged support of Palestine Action.

About 2,500 have been charged, although none have gone to trial as a legal challenge to the proscription continues and will be heard by the Supreme Court next month.

Netflix to shed 1,000 jobs in steepest cuts since 2022

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 16:47:32 GMT

Netflix has said it will cut 1,000 jobs globally as the streaming giant faces competition from rivals.

The cuts - the steepest since 2022 - will mainly affect its human resources, legal, product and technology teams and equates to around 6% of its total workforce.

Last month, Netflix 's co-chief executive, Ted Sarandos, told the Bloomberg Screentime summit that the company was "not growing as fast as I want us to".

The streaming industry is going through a period of consolidation following Paramount's $110bn (£83.6bn) merger with Warner Bros, the owner of HBO, while free platforms such as YouTube and TikTok continue to attract viewers.

In an email to staff, Sarandos and co-chief executive Greg Peters said Netflix would "continue to invest aggressively in high-impact areas to improve our service and further drive growth and innovation".

Netflix had come close to its own deal with Warner Bros but was outbid in the end by Paramount.

Commenting on the "organisational changes", Sarandos and Peters said: "Our mission - to entertain the world - is ambitious, and delivering on it requires us to be disciplined about where we prioritise our people and resources."

Managers will tell employees whether their job has been cut "soon".

The email added that the firm was on track to meet its financial forecasts. The streaming service is due to report its latest results on 20 October.

Investors have been weighing up user engagement - the amount of time customers spend on Netflix - as it competes with platforms such as YouTube and TikTok.

The firm has continued to commission shows - its most popular at the moment is "Below", an underwater mystery series.

It has also been attempting to expand its business, including gaming and allowing adverts to play before and during shows and films.

However, "they haven't been able to find another line of major revenue," said Ross Benes, senior analyst at eMarketer.

He added that the company remains reliant on a subscription business that could be approaching a plateau.

"Eventually, because they're nearing a ceiling with that, they'll need another revenue stream to diversify, and that's where it's been more of a struggle," he said.

The last major redundancy round at Netflix was in 2022, when it cut about 450 jobs after losing subscribers for the first time in more than a decade.

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 16:37:59 GMT

Andy Burnham has said he will not support his party forming a partnership with the Green Party as long as it maintains a policy of defining Zionism as a form of racism.

Speaking in the House of Commons, the prime minister said the motion, backed by Green members at its party conference earlier this month, was "wrong" and "antisemitic" and Labour would have "nothing to do with it".

The motion said Zionism was an ideology supporting "an ethnonationalist Jewish state in historic Palestine to the exclusion and/ or domination of the non-Jewish population".

It was criticised by Jewish groups, as well as four Green MPs and two former leaders, one of whom said it was "dangerous" and "divisive".

Party leader Zack Polanski has said the motion was "imperfect" and that he had wanted to see it amended.

However, he has defended the substantial part of the motion arguing that "the displacement and genocide of the Palestinian people is clearly underpinned by racist ideology" was "obvious". Israel has repeatedly denied its actions in Gaza amount to genocide.

The motion, having been backed by members, has become party policy but will not necessarily be included in the party's manifesto at the next general election.

The subject was raised during Prime Minister's Questions, when Conservative MP Dame Harriett Baldwin urged Burnham to "rule out co-operating" with the Green Party.

Burnham replied: "Can I condemn the motion that was passed unequivocally.

"It is wrong, it is antisemitic, and this side of the House will have nothing to do with it.

"We will always take the right response when it comes to any partnerships we have with other parties.

"We cannot support a partnership with a party that holds the position that has recently been adopted."

In response to Burnham's comments, Polanski said: "I'm the only Jewish leader of a political party. I'm a victim of antisemitism every day and I've made it clear Jewish people are welcome in my party.

"You're a prime minister who arms a genocide and locks pensioners up for opposing it."

He added that the "blurring of challenging Israel and antisemitism" was "dangerous and irresponsible".

Labour is currently in coalition with the Green Party on local councils in Sheffield, Lewes and Warwick. In more than 10 other areas, Labour run the council with the Greens as part of a broader coalition.

A Downing Street spokesman would not confirm if the prime minister's position extended to local areas or just applied to national government level.

The adoption of the motion has prompted some Green Party figures to call for changes to the party's rules.

Last week, MP Ellie Chowns told the BBC: "I think it is clear that if we have policy, such significant and damaging policy, made by a vote of just 0.4% of our members, that something isn't working in our structures and our policy-making process."

Members of the Green Party of England and Wales endorsed the policy motion by 1,022 votes to 813 - a small fraction of the 230,000 members in the party.

Key backer of the motion Lubna Speitan has defended the motion arguing that it aimed to oppose "racist systems of oppression" adding: "We stand united against all forms of racism and discrimination - no exemptions."

Fifa to offer extra £2.6bn for football development

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 15:59:59 GMT

Fifa president Gianni Infantino will be standing for re-election in March

Fifa is set to propose a $3.4bn (£2.6bn) increase in the amount of money it hands out for football development across the world.

World football's governing body wants to release the money from its reserves in a move which would more than double the level of planned distribution funding for 2027-2030.

It says it has been able to release the extra cash because of the commercial success of this summer's World Cup.

The proposal will be made at a meeting of Fifa's council on Thursday and comes at a time when the organisation is under pressure following president Gianni Infantino's aborted Fifa Forward Enterprise (FFE) plan.

Infantino wanted to sell stakes in the World Cup and other competitions to private investors but faced huge criticism for the proposal, with Uefa, Concacaf and the Asian Football Confederation questioning his position.

Uefa and Concacaf wrote to Infantino in September calling on Fifa to pay at least $10m (£7.5m) from its cash reserves to each of the 211 member associations.

In a letter then sent to Fifa's six continental confederations, Infantino - who will be standing for re-election for a fourth time in March 2027 - said he would support "the greatest level of additional funding that can responsibly be delivered".

Fifa will now offer extra money from its reserves in addition to the current $2.7bn (£2.05bn) budgeted to be paid to all 211 member associations, the confederations, regional associations and certain non-Fifa affiliated associations.

In total, Fifa will now be spending $6.35bn (£4.83bn) on football development for the 2027-2030 cycle, with a focus on investment and development of women's and youth football.

The breakdown is as follows:

• $25m (£19m) per member association

• $99m (£75m) for each of the six confederations

• $8.25m (£6.3m) for each of the 13 zonal/regional associations

• $1.1m (£836,000) for 100 member associations for travel and equipment solidarity

The terms of the distribution would be as follows:

• One third would be allocated to women's and youth football development

• One third would be allocated to infrastructure (of which there shall be equal access to women's and youth football)

• One third would be allocated to projects

The plan, including the eligibility, application, approval, disbursement, control and audit requirements, will be finalised by the Fifa council at its next meeting in December 2026.

It will all be subject to approval by the 77th Fifa congress in March 2027.

BBC Sport understands there is a proposal on the table at the council to change the disciplinary code and prevent a repeat of the Folarin Balogun situation, an unprecedented scenario which meant the US striker did not miss a game after being sent off in the World Cup.

If approved, it will be expressly noted in the regulations that an automatic one-match ban for a red card cannot be suspended.

Questions have been raised over the process which led to Balogun having his ban suspended.

US President Donald Trump said he called Infantino to get the ban rescinded, which raised serious concerns over political interference.

A proposal has been put forward to add to Article 27 of the regulations a list of instances whereby a ban cannot be suspended.

This would include an automatic one-game ban for a red card.

Toilet paper and secret recorders: What we know about the arrest of the Saudi OpenAI engineer

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 15:54:04 GMT

A Saudi national who worked for OpenAI appeared in court this week for allegedly planting a secret recording device behind a TV in one of the tech giant's meeting rooms.

Abdulrahman Alfozan, a 35-year-old engineer, was arrested by the FBI and charged with using or attempting to use a device "to intercept oral communications".

As yet, it is not clear what he was planning to do with the recordings.

But, here's we do know so far.

The engineer asked AI to help him listen in to discussions about AI, according to an official complaint (the document filed in court).

Between 17 August and 16 September, Alfozan had reportedly asked a productivity AI assistant several questions about meeting rooms and their availability.

Some detailed in the complaint include "how about external partners, where would they usually meet with OpenAI at" and "can you list today's meeting in this room".

The complaint also claimed that in September, Alfozan used a chatbot associated with his spouse to ask how to build and install a recording device and how to access it remotely.

Alfozan had worked at the firm for around a year before he allegedly made his attempt to plant a recorder.

The room where the device was found was regularly used to hold meetings to discuss "highly confidential technical, commercial and strategic information" as well as OpenAI's "government engagements and commercial relationships", the complaint stated.

Alfozan had no authority to access or be involved with meetings in the room, the complaint states.

But CCTV footage recorded him near the conference room on eight separate occasions in September.

On 30 September, OpenAI staff discovered a "surreptitious audio recording device" behind a television - which had been wrapped in toilet paper.

A camera was placed in the conference room and on 5 October, Alfozan was filmed entering the room and appeared to look behind the television where the device had been inserted.

The complaint also stated that same day he allegedly left an unattended bag in a different room.

When security personnel opened it, it was found to contain "another recording device" similar to the one discovered, as well as Alfozan's wallet and ID.

In a post on X, OpenAI wrote it had used its own AI models to "understand the device and where it had come from".

It also said: "At this time, we have no indications of this individual inappropriately accessing or exfiltrating model weights, source code, or customer data."

Alfozan had been employed as an engineer by OpenAI since July 2025. The complaint said he was educated in the US and has been a legal permanent resident since 2020.

He was arrested by the FBI on 9 October and his contract with OpenAI has been terminated.

Alfozan's lawyer William Pernik told the BBC: "Like countless Americans, Mr Alfozan came to this country years ago as a young person with a dream.

"These allegations appear wholly out of character for him, and we will relentlessly investigate all evidence produced by the government in this case to understand what actually happened, who was responsible and any possible reasons why."

Pernik added: "In the meantime, we respectfully ask everyone to withhold judgment until full facts become known. Like every one of us, Mr Alfozan is presumed innocent, and what has been made public is an accusation, not a finding of guilt."

On Tuesday, Alfozan made his initial appearance in a federal court in California. A detention hearing is scheduled on 19 October to determine whether he should remain in custody while proceedings continue.

The FBI investigation is ongoing.

If convicted, Alfozan faces a maximum penalty of five years in prison and a $250,000 (£190,000) fine.

Container ship hit by projectile sinks in Strait of Hormuz, Singapore says

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 15:34:19 GMT

A Singapore-flagged container ship has sunk after being struck by an unknown projectile while transiting the Strait of Hormuz, according to Singaporean authorities.

All 21 crew members of GFS Pride were evacuated and no injuries were reported after the attack on Tuesday, which caused damage and a fire, the Maritime and Port Authority of Singapore said.

Another Singapore-flagged container vessel, GFS Giselle, sustained hull damage after being hit by a projectile on Wednesday but was able to reach a safe anchorage with all 22 crew, it added.

Iran's Revolutionary Guards said a ship hit naval mines and sank while passing through what they called an "unauthorised" route in the Strait of Hormuz.

The Guards released photos that appeared to show a plume of black smoke rising from a container vessel that was still afloat but down by the stern. In other photos, dozens of containers and their contents could be seen floating in the water.

The Hydrographer of Pakistan warned mariners that GFS Pride had sunk 4 nautical miles (7.4km) north-west of Ras al-Sheikh Masud in Oman's Musandam Peninsula.

Meanwhile, the United Kingdom Maritime Trade Operations (UKMTO) agency said that two unnamed container ships had reported being struck by unknown projectiles while transiting the Strait of Hormuz between 16:40 and 17:00 GMT on Tuesday.

Both vessels sustained minor damage and were able to continue their passages, it added.

Merchant vessels have come under repeated attack in the Strait of Hormuz - which connects the Gulf to the Gulf of Oman and Arabian Sea - and elsewhere in the Middle East since the US and Israel's war with Iran began in February.

At least 24 seafarers have been killed in 106 confirmed incidents, according to the UN's International Maritime Organization.

The Malta-flagged container ship Safeen Prestige, which was attacked near Oman on 4 March, is the only other vessel reported to have sunk in that time.

However, maritime risk management group Vanguard said on Wednesday that the Antigua and Barbuda-flagged bulk carrier Cape Dao was reported to be sinking after an unsuccessful rescue attempt, according to Reuters news agency.

One seafarer was killed when the Cape Dao's bridge and accommodation area were hit by an unknown projectile as it transited through the Strait of Hormuz, north-east of Khasab, Oman, on 23 September.

The Strait of Hormuz, through which a fifth of global oil and gas shipments usually pass, was effectively closed after the start of the Iran war due to Iranian missile and drone attacks on commercial vessels and a US naval blockade of Iranian ports.

The US and Iran reached a preliminary agreement to end the war and reopen the strait in June, but it collapsed within weeks after Iranian attacks on shipping resumed and the US reinstated its blockade.

Iran insisted it would not give up some measure of control over the waterway and began targeting vessels using a southern route through Omani waters that avoids its preferred route through Iranian waters.

To keep exports flowing from Gulf Arab states, the US military has been providing protection for tankers shuttling oil through the Strait of Hormuz.

Last month, crude exports through the waterway rose to around 80% of pre-war levels of 16 million barrels per day (bpd) on average, according to analysts from shipping data company Kpler, despite an increase in attacks on shipping.

US inflation rises more than expected - here's what is happening with prices

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 15:26:14 GMT

US inflation rose by more than expected in the year to September, according to official figures, in the final data release before the midterm elections next month.

The pace of price rises increased to 3.7%, up from 3.4% in the 12 months to August.

Inflation was above forecasts, driven by higher petrol prices, which have risen following disruption to Middle East energy supplies due to the US-Israel conflict with Iran.

The elections are being fought over cost-of-living pressures across the US.

Inflation is the rate at which the price of products or services increases over time.

For example, if a bottle of milk costs $1 but rises to $1.10 a year later, then annual inflation for bottled milk is 10%.

Officials from the Bureau of Labor Statistics measure inflation through the consumer price index (CPI).

It tracks the price changes of hundreds of day-to-day items including fresh food, gasoline and medical services to work out the overall level of inflation in the US economy.

The bureau updates this so-called basket of goods over time as peoples' spending and lifestyle habits change.

There are several factors that can cause inflation to rise.

If demand for a product outstrips its supply, that can lead to producers raising prices - the craze for Labubu dolls in recent years and the high resale prices are an example.

Higher incomes can also lead to higher demand, for example, which in turn pushes up prices.

Supply can also affect inflation. If supply of a product is reduced, but demand remains constant, that can cause prices to rise. A good example of this is crude oil, which is used to make gasoline and diesel.

Before the US-Israel war with Iran began, it was trading at just over $70 a barrel, but when the conflict constricted crude supplies, the price quickly rose and is currently over $100.

Inflation has risen to 3.7% in the year to September, which is a higher-than-expected increase fuelled by petrol prices.

Vehicle fuel prices jumped by 34.2% over 12 months.

In September alone, they jumped by 9.1%, accounting for more than half of the increase in inflation, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics.

Overall, energy inflation was up by 20.4% over the year as prices for fuel oil, energy services and electricity climbed. Fuel oil, used in home heating, was up 74.2% over the year.

Experts said the US Federal Reserve - which sets the country's interest rate - will be closely watching to see if energy costs begin to affect other areas of the economy, such as food prices, and drive inflation higher.

At the moment, food inflation remains subdued. It was flat over the month of September, and was 2.5% across the year.

Core inflation, which strips out food and energy prices, was 2.6%.

Shortly after the latest inflation figure was released, the Social Security Administration announced that Social Security benefits including old age, survivors and disability insurance would rise by a similar amount - 3.6% - in 2027.

The administration said that the 3.6% rise will mean an average retirement benefit increase of $75 a month.

The cost-of-living adjustment will take effect in January for about 72 million people, while increased payments for the 7.2m receiving the supplemental security income will start on 31 December.

Since coming into office for the second time, President Donald Trump has introduced tariffs on most US trading partners around the world.

While most of the additional global levies were outlawed by the Supreme Court in February, the president immediately announced a new 10% global tariff to replace them.

Trump argued the tariffs would increase America's tax earnings, boost manufacturing and encourage people to buy US-made goods, but the higher import taxes have also had an impact on inflation, according to research from the Federal Reserve Bank of New York.

Higher tariffs - which are levies that businesses pay on the goods they import - directly raise prices for US companies unless the overseas firms they are buying from lower prices to absorb some of the cost.

In response to the new tariffs, the New York Fed found exporters reduced prices very little, meaning US-based businesses ended up absorbing nearly 90% of the cost of the added tax.

Indirectly, higher tariffs also affected the prices of locally made goods, as many US producers rely on parts or materials from overseas.

Overall, the Fed found that every percentage point increase in average tariffs led to nearly a 0.25% price increase for consumer goods after one year.

While inflation is currently above where the US Federal Reserve would like - it has a target of 2% - it has been much higher in previous years.

During the Covid pandemic, issues such as supply chain disruption started pushing inflation up.

Additionally, in early 2022 when Russia launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine, oil prices surged over $100 a barrel.

Both of these factors helped to drive inflation to a peak of about 9%.

It's important to note that if inflation is rising at a slower rate, that does not mean prices are going down - it simply means prices are rising at a slower pace.

A bit of inflation is considered good for an economy as the the Federal Reserve's target rate illustrates.

Many central banks consider inflation of about 2% or a little higher to be stable, and at a level that doesn't impact people's purchasing decisions.

Much below that, people can delay spending if they expect price rises to slow further, or to fall. That can lead to businesses reducing wages, or reducing the number of workers to cope with lower demand.

Australia's first-ever Michelin stars go to 11 restaurants in one state

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 13:35:29 GMT

Australia has been awarded its first Michelin stars, with 11 restaurants in the state of South Australia receiving the distinction.

Magill Estate Restaurant in Adelaide was the only venue to receive two stars, while 10 others were awarded one star each.

South Australia's government paid for incognito inspectors to visit the state, according to national broadcaster ABC, which is why no spots have been recognised in other parts of the country.

The announcement comes more than a century after the Michelin Guide was first published in France and following years of unsuccessful attempts to bring it to Australia.

Michelin has approached the national tourism board about bringing the guide to the country more than once, with no luck.

South Australia decided to go it alone and pursued its own deal. The state government has not disclosed how much it paid, but Tourism Minister Emily Bourke defended the investment in an interview with ABC News.

"It is enabling us to create a tourism destination and put us on the world map," she said.

The move follows Michelin's expansion into New Zealand, which became the first destination in Oceania to feature in the guide in July, agreeing to pay NZ$6.3m (£2.7m; $3.5m) over three years for its inclusion.

Created by brothers André and Édouard Michelin in 1900, the guide was originally designed to encourage motorists to travel - ultimately boosting their core business of selling tyres. It offered drivers maps and recommendations for places to eat and stay, before evolving into one of the best-known names in restaurant recognition.

Michelin stars were introduced in 1926 and have since become a major distinction for chefs and restaurants around the world.

One star recognises high-quality cooking, two stars indicate excellent cooking worth a detour, while three stars are reserved for exceptional cuisine worth a special journey.

Among the one-star recipients is Arkhé in Norwood, which specialises in open-fire cooking, while Aptos in Adelaide serves a tasting menu focused on native Australian ingredients. Here is the full list:

Two Michelin stars

One Michelin star

The wider guide includes more than 70 other South Australian restaurants, with some recognised through the Bib Gourmand category, which highlights restaurants offering good food at more moderate prices.

Burnham had two hospitality dinners with Man City chairman, deleted records show

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 12:47:43 GMT

Andy Burnham had two hospitality dinners with Manchester City football club's chairman while mayor of Greater Manchester, it has emerged.

The PM dined with Khaldoon Al Mubarak at matches in 2017 and 2018 - but this was not detailed in a statement on Monday by the Greater Manchester Combined Authority (GMCA) about his meetings with the club.

Burnham has been under pressure to clarify his previous discussions with Man City since the club was found guilty of having "systematically" broken Premier League rules, which it will appeal.

The PM, who was mayor until June, sparked widespread controversy when he said last month he would be "really concerned" if the club's owners now sold up.

On Monday, the GMCA released a statement about Burnham's meetings with Man City in his role as mayor for the region for almost a decade.

The statement said he had a formal meeting with Al Mubarak in October 2021 at the UK Government's Global Investment Summit, arranged by the Department for International Trade; and that he met the club chairman "informally in the stands" at a testimonial match in 2019 for former player Vincent Kompany.

It also said that "in his capacity as mayor, he was a guest of Manchester City at home matches on a small number of occasions".

But the statement did not specify that this referred to two hospitality dinners he had with the Manchester City chairman at separate matches.

A previous GMCA register of declarations for Burnham, seen by the BBC, detailed a "dinner with [then-Manchester City Council leader] Richard Leese and Chair of Man City" for an Everton match at the Etihad stadium in August 2017, valued at £300 for hospitality.

Below is another entry for "dinner with the Chairman at the Man City/Man Utd derby at the Etihad" in April 2018, valued at approximately £699.

Burnham previously declared those dinners, alongside a £150 pair of tickets to see Ed Sheeran perform at the stadium in May 2018.

But the register of declarations has since been deleted from the GMCA website, as first reported by The Sun. The BBC understands it is the authority's policy to only publish entries from the current mayoral term.

BBC News has asked GMCA for more details about this policy - given its website has entries dating back to 2022, despite the current mayoral term beginning in 2024.

Downing Street has defended the two dinners with Man City's chairman, saying this was hospitality received at home games, which had been declared, rather than separate meetings with the club chairman.

But political pressure on Burnham to be clearer about any former discussions with Man City is continuing to mount.

Tory shadow sports minister Louie French said: "Andy Burnham promised the country and House of Commons full transparency on his dealings with Manchester City.

"This was essential after his inappropriate intervention into football."

And Reform UK's Robert Jenrick said "this saga stinks" as he accused Burnham of having apparently "concealed his extensive links to Man City's owners".

Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch has demanded for several days that No 10 release all details of meetings between the prime minister and senior figures at the club.

Burnham told the Rest Is Politics podcast last week he was "happy" to do this and would "quite clearly put out there everything that I have done working with them in my previous role as Mayor of Greater Manchester".

"None of it is recent, actually, in terms of the meetings I had. I only had one significant meeting, actually, and that was in 2021."

Asked at Prime Minister's Questions on Wednesday whether anyone at the club had raised the charges they were facing with him or his team, Burnham said: "They did not raise those issues with me."

With the PM continuing to face pressure on the issue, he told MPs he was happy for "everything to be set out in full", referring to the GMCA's statement as "a full statement of my key dealings with the owners of Manchester City Football Club".

When asked by reporters why the statement did not refer to the two dinners, and what the difference is between "everything" and "key dealings", the PM's press secretary referred back to the GMCA statement.

The list of meetings, which was published by the GMCA on Monday, did not include detail on what was discussed. The BBC has asked GMCA why the two dinners were not specifically mentioned.

In September Man City - which has dominated the top flight of English football for years - was found guilty by an independent commission of breaching Premier League financial regulations.

The commission found City had broken rules over a nine-year period between 2009 and 2018 by creating "sham" contracts with commercial partners to secretly funnel more than £830m into their football operations.

Sanctions are yet to be announced and City, which denies any wrongdoing, has lodged an appeal against the verdict.

Earlier this month, Sir Richard Leese - who was head of Manchester City Council for 25 years until 2021 - defended a decade-old deal between the council and Man City owners Abu Dhabi United Group (ADUG).

Under the partnership between the authority and the company, which is linked to the United Arab Emirates royal family, land was leased to ADUG at "below comparable rates", according to a University of Sheffield report.

Family pays tribute to 'warm and engaging' man killed in tiger attack

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 12:44:17 GMT

The family of a man who was killed by a tiger after he entered its enclosure at a wildlife park has paid tribute, describing him as "bright, warm and engaging".

William Chapman, 23, visited Yorkshire Wildlife Park near Doncaster on Saturday and was attacked after going into the animal's pen. He was pronounced dead at the scene.

In a statement, his family said that he had suffered from "significant mental illness" over a number of years and had been prescribed "strong medication".

His family added: "It will be for the coroner to decide what part this played in his death. We would ask everybody to respect that process."

City of Doncaster Council has launched a health and safety investigation into Chapman's death.

The park remains closed and a reopening date has not yet been confirmed.

"William was a bright, warm and engaging young man whom his family dearly loved - and William loved them back," a statement released on Tuesday evening said.

The family also asked for privacy, saying "the publication and circulation of deeply offensive media content is causing them severe distress at an exceptionally vulnerable time".

"The family are utterly devastated by their loss and are struggling to come to terms with what has happened," the statement continued.

"The family are hopeful that the relevant authorities will do their upmost to get to the bottom of why our amazing son did what he did."

A spokesperson for South Yorkshire Police said Chapman's family were being supported by specialist officers.

An inquest is due to open into the death at Doncaster Coroner's Court on Thursday.

Footage of the incident has been widely shared on social media, including on Tuesday evening by US President Donald Trump.

Downing Street said people should respect the family's views on sharing footage of the incident, but declined to comment on Trump's decision to post the video.

The prime minister's official spokesman said: "I think obviously the family have made their position clear. Of course, we respect that position.

"I'm not going to comment on the president's social media channels, he's got a press secretary who can speak to that."

Also commenting on the sharing of videos on social media and Trump's posts, South Yorkshire Mayor Oliver Coppard said his office had been in contact with Chapman's family on Tuesday, with his relatives "sharing the pain and suffering it's causing them knowing that videos and pictures of his death are being shared across social media".

Coppard also urged people not to speculate about the incident online.

"That speculation adds to the pain being endured by Mr Chapman's family," he said.

"Answers will come in time."

The incident, which took place shortly after 13:30 BST on Saturday, led to the evacuation and ongoing closure of the park.

An eyewitness described seeing Chapman wearing headphones and "pacing around" near the enclosure before he went in.

Mobile phone footage, believed to be from Chapman, appears to show the inside of the enclosure as he approaches the tiger across a patch of grass.

Chapman was a former pupil of the Jackie Palmer Stage School in High Wycombe.

"We loved watching William grow, both as a person and as a performer, during his time with us," the school said in a tribute on Tuesday.

"It was a privilege to be part of his journey, and he will be remembered with great fondness by everyone who had the pleasure of knowing him."

"Our thoughts and heartfelt condolences are with William's family and friends during this incredibly difficult time," the statement continued.

As a child, Chapman appeared in the ITV production May Contain Nuts alongside Shirley Henderson - who is best known for playing Moaning Myrtle in the Harry Potter films - and Darren Boyd.

He also portrayed Edward Jenner in the BBC series True Stories.

Staff at Yorkshire Wildlife Park have separately said their "thoughts and heartfelt sympathies" are with Chapman's family and confirmed the rare Amur tiger, called Altai, remained in the enclosure and would not be euthanised.

Dan Swaine, the local authority's executive director of place, said the council was "deeply saddened" by the incident and sent its "deepest sympathies" to Chapman's family and those who were at the scene.

Pope's cancer diagnosis leaves Catholics questioning what comes next

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 12:22:17 GMT

Vatican transparency about papal health has come a long way from the secrecy of the past.

But that does not mean Catholics were prepared for such seismic news about cancer and removal of a large section of Pope Leo XIV's lung - even after he announced on Sunday that doctors had discovered a nodule.

On Tuesday, the 71-year-old was diagnosed with lung adenocarcinoma following 90-minute surgery to remove a lower lobe of his right lung.

Part of the shock has, of course, been because there had been few signs until now that concern was needed about any imminent risks to the Pope's health.

A member of the Order of St Augustine, a saint who placed great importance on developing good habits, the Pope is known to place great importance in good habits, including when it comes to health and fitness, such as weekly games of tennis.

Now, with so many unknowns about the pontiff's prognosis and what treatment he might need, questions swirl around what may have to change in his routine, and for how long.

Certainly, given the dangers surrounding air travel soon after lung surgery, and the high altitude of some of the stops involved, a November trip to South America – including to Peru, the country Pope Leo called home for more than 20 years – may be in doubt.

His predecessor, Francis, once wrote that he had started his papacy thinking he might only have a few years in the role, having already suffered health complications throughout his life.

As such, it was clear that he was intent on making waves quickly and implementing what some saw as fairly radical change in the Church at a swift pace.

While Pope Leo's papacy has undoubtedly started in what appears to be a comparatively measured way, he has still grown quickly into being a powerful, uncompromising and compassionate moral voice on the world stage.

His messaging in opposition to war, in support of the dignity of migrants and on the dangers of AI in particular has stood out in its clarity.

To some extent, he has already done what many Catholics had feared was barely possible, in starting to bring closer together different factions of a Church that had seemed somewhat divided.

Noticeably, both progressive and traditionalist followers often talking of him as a voice that reflects their own.

But even after successful surgery, and with no details yet about what happens now, speculation has already begun as to whether a cancer diagnosis will mean an evident shift in Pope Leo's pace in implementing changes he sees as necessary.

On the other hand any dramatic moves, such as on doctrine, would certainly feel out of character for a Pope who for nearly a year and a half has shown himself to be measured, thoughtful and discerning, as well as having a focus on stability.

Civil servant faked illness with make-up to juggle five jobs, court hears

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 11:57:22 GMT

A civil servant is accused of earning almost £350,000 by juggling five full-time public sector roles - using sick notes and working from home to cope with the workload.

Kashim Chowdhury allegedly held multiple government jobs between February 2020 and September 2023, earning £350,000 overall.

The jury at Southwark Crown Court has been told he wore white make-up during a video call with his boss to try to look ill, but the make-up began peeling off his skin.

Chowdury, a 55-year-old from east London, is on trial accused of nine counts of fraud, which he denies. He denies acting dishonestly or intending to make a dishonest gain.

The jury was told that Chowdhury got jobs by making ''false representations'' about his employment status whilst applying. Over three years he held overlapping jobs at Tower Hamlets Council in east London, the Home Office, the Department for Environment, Food and Rural Affairs (Defra), the UK Health Security Agency and the Department of Health and Social Care.

The jury was told that in 2023 his manager at the Health Security Agency, Aleesha Adams, organised a video call with him to discuss his sick leave.

Ms Adams claims Chowdhury was wearing make-up on his face to appear ill - and that during the meeting the make-up started to separate on his forehead showing his real skin underneath. At the time of the call he is alleged to have been paid for five full time jobs.

Lucy Organ, for the prosecution, told the court: "There could be no doubt about it, the defendant had applied make-up to his face for this Teams meeting to try and pretend to his line manager that he was unwell.

"This almost laughable incident demonstrates, we say, quite how far the defendant was willing to go in his deceit."

Prosecutors say Chowdhury "exploited" the work from home culture that began during the Covid pandemic. The court was told that Chowdhury had told his employers that he had to work from home to care for his "unwell" mother.

But the prosecutor, Ms Organ, said his workload eventually became "too much to cope with" and his performance suffered.

The court heard that he was signed off work from some jobs due to "mixed anxiety and a depressive disorder", but he did not hand his sick note to all his employers.

Jurors heard that the alleged fraud came to light after the National Fraud Initiative alerted Defra to them.

The trial continues.

'I applied for 200 jobs' - How 'spray and pray' AI job applications can backfire

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 08:27:17 GMT

Would you use AI to help you write a CV or cover letter? How about during an online assessment or video interview for a job?

We are increasingly using the technology to help us with job applications, graduates and recruiters say, with many employers happy for us to do so within reason.

But when does it cross a line from being acceptable to unethical, or even cheating?

Tom, 22, from Maidstone graduated last year with a geography degree and says he applied for over 200 jobs before finally landing a role last month as a restaurant manager.

He says the process was "exhausting" and "demoralising", with employers often not even getting back to him, and he wouldn't have got through it without some help from AI.

"Employers want you to put your absolute best into the application, but if I'm applying for 10 to 20 jobs in a day, the reality is you can't do that."

While Tom says he never used the technology in an assessment or interview, he did use it to help him with cover letters and CVs or to find the right "buzz words" that early stage screening in a recruitment process will pick up on.

He also thinks it's "hypocritical" for companies to ask candidates not to use AI when some are using it themselves to screen candidates.

"That's where it feels like a weird tightrope. You can't use AI too much because otherwise you're not human. But if you don't write like an AI, you may get skipped by the AI screening."

This summer, the Institute of Student Employers (ISE) surveyed 155 of the UK's biggest graduate employers and found they were becoming "more accepting" of candidates using AI "at some stages of the recruitment process".

According to the trade group, the most commonly accepted use was writing cover letters or CVs, followed by completing online application questions.

However, it also found 92% of employers had encountered or suspected AI "misconduct" in the job selection process - up from 85% last year. Most commonly this included using AI as a real-time assistant during online video interviews or to complete online assessments.

On top of this, the tech is helping candidates write multiple applications at the same time - something known in the industry as "spray and pray". Stephen Isherwood, joint chief executive of the ISE, says it is "driving up the competition and pressure on students" in an already competitive graduate recruitment market.

Hollie Moody, head of early careers recruitment at energy giant EDF, says she doesn't blame graduates for using AI in applications given how "brutal" the jobs market is right now. But she does think it's becoming a problem.

EDF opened its graduate recruitment scheme three weeks ago, but decided to close it early after receiving 12,000 applications - a 10% increase on last year.

Moody says AI almost certainly contributed to this, adding that the tech is being used "a lot more freely" in the actual selection process.

"What we don't have is a level playing field, because what you'll find is that some candidates will absolutely use AI throughout the whole process, and other candidates won't."

Like many firms, EDF now issues guidance on the appropriate use of AI in job applications, and is trying to make its recruitment process as "AI-proof" as possible.

It only reads CVs in the later stages of the hiring process and uses skills and behaviour-based online assessments and video interviews to better assess candidates.

But Moody says some applicants still try to bend the rules, particularly in video interviews.

"A lot of the answers [we hear] are the same. Or people might pause whilst they wait for AI to come back to them with an answer. It's almost like people are reading a script."

One recently employed trainee accountant, who didn't want us to use his real name, told the BBC he'd applied for 50 graduate jobs and used AI in every application. But he says it was only for researching roles, tidying up his CV and generating mock practise questions for video interviews.

While he says he would never use the tech to cheat on assessments he knows people who would.

"I know people who have applied for 100 jobs and after a while they become desperate. If they see everyone else is using AI, they think how can I compete?"

According to the ISE, the graduate market has stabilised after a sharp fall last year but remains tough. Graduate roles currently attract an average of 141 applications each, it says.

And while only 16% of employers it surveyed are using AI in the recruitment process, 29% plan to introduce it within the next year.

Hollie Moody says EDF does not currently use AI in its recruitment process but may have to in future.

For her, the use of AI in job applications remains a "moral dilemma" that employers and candidates will continue to grapple with. But she stresses it cannot replace what her firm is looking for in new recruits.

"It's about authenticity, but also personality. We want diversity of thought in the business."

Correction 14 October: This article has been amended to show that according to the ISE's survey 29% of employers plan to introduce AI in the recruitment process over the next year, rather than more than half as we originally reported.

One stolen every four minutes - inside the police battle against vehicle thieves

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 05:01:07 GMT

It is 01:00 on a Thursday and we are in the back of an unmarked police car on the hunt for vehicle thieves in east London.

A call comes in over the radio about a stolen Ford Transit van. Within 25 minutes officers have found it, chased it down and arrested a suspect.

Officers tell us the night has been successful, with further arrests made, yet across the UK a car is stolen every four minutes.

Despite a recent drop, vehicle thefts are up almost 50% in England and Wales compared with a decade ago, according to Home Office data. Last year, just 3% of vehicle theft offences resulted in a charge or summons.

As well as spending time on the front line in London and Sussex, we have privately spoken to 20 police officers from six forces.

They told us while they have the skills and experience to tackle vehicle crime, they are overstretched, under-resourced and struggling to keep pace with increasingly sophisticated criminal networks.

Thieves are becoming more confident. A man who claims he receives stolen vehicles and breaks them into parts at a so-called "chop shop" told the BBC he does not fear the authorities, because they "couldn't catch a cold".

Once the preserve of joyriders and opportunists, vehicle theft is now a lucrative global criminal business. Cars are stolen to order, and moved across the country or dismantled within hours, say police, making them difficult to recover and suspects hard to track. Many vehicles are sent overseas to places like the United Arab Emirates, The Democratic Republic of Congo and Jamaica.

Victims of car theft have told us they feel frustrated and helpless. One woman said it took car thieves just 35 seconds to steal her BMW, yet police only began investigating a day later. Another man said he feared for his life when masked thieves broke into his home demanding his car keys.

The success criminals are having is also hitting motorists more widely, with insurance quotes having risen by 82% since 2021, according to security think tank the Royal United Services Institute (Rusi).

Our night-time ride in the unmarked police car takes place during an eight-hour Metropolitan Police operation targeting vehicle crime.

"Havering has been affected quite badly," PC Yoosef Outaleb tells us - partly because thieves use the back roads as a cut-through to neighbouring Essex.

Police say the county has particularly high numbers of illegal "chop shops", which dismantle vehicles for parts so they can be sold and exported.

"They [the criminals] don't have borders, whereas we do," he says. When criminal activity straddles police force boundaries, investigations can often slow down - which gangs look to exploit.

Two stolen vehicles are recovered, yet Met officers also uncover other crimes.

They pursue armed robbers on e-bikes, apprehend a drunk driver and arrest three men in a car full of Class A drugs, cash and phones.

It is all important work, but police admit that responding to these crimes diverts them away from targeting known vehicle thieves.

The thieves, known as "grafters" within criminal networks, are out every night stealing cars, the man claiming to work at a chop shop told us.

Many vehicles are then broken into parts. Such is the demand, he says, that deals to re-sell the parts are often done before he has even finished dismantling a vehicle.

Some stolen vehicles are left intact, given new number plates and sold in the UK or abroad, while others are used to facilitate drug dealing and human trafficking, or as getaway vehicles for robberies and break-ins.

While the long-term trend presents difficult reading for police, reported vehicle thefts fell 5% in England and Wales last year, to 119,000. In Scotland and Northern Ireland, vehicle thefts remained largely flat, at about 5,000 and 1,200 a year respectively.

Fifteen of the 32 police forces in England and Wales that responded to a BBC Freedom of Information request said they do not employ any full-time, specially-trained stolen vehicle examiners, who can carry out detailed examinations of cars and bolster investigations.

But Assistant Chief Constable Sarah Grahame, the national police lead on tackling vehicle crime, tells us officers' efforts - like the night-time operation in east London - are having an impact.

UK forces are also collaborating with Interpol, shipping companies and vehicle manufacturers to address an issue "that spans globally", she says.

She admits current theft levels are unacceptable, but says there is no funding to routinely carry out large-scale operations within current police budgets, set by the Home Office.

Instead, forces organise "intensification weeks" aimed at disrupting criminal networks.

One involving 32 forces last month, called Operation Alliances, led to at least 286 arrests and 319 vehicles seized.

In East Sussex in September, where we witnessed a smaller, targeted operation, we were told that 17 cars had been stolen in the Hastings and Rother areas in the previous two months.

Many of the vehicles were keyless Toyota RAV4s - taken by thieves using technology that bypasses vehicle security systems. Three of the vehicles had been taken the previous night.

Within hours, officers recovered all three vehicles and arrested three suspects, including one they believe had been in charge of the other two.

Two suspects have now been charged, including one arrested after a stolen Toyota was found near his home.

Sussex vehicle crime lead Ch Insp Oliver Fisher, says police resources are now being focused on "the top of that criminal network... that's where you'll seize the most assets and cause the most disruption".

Without catching criminal bosses, experts agree, others lower down will continue carrying out thefts to order - often using tech such as cloned keys to access vehicles.

Manufacturers need "a constant focus on security", says Thatcham Research - a body that works with carmakers, police and government to improve vehicle security.

"Within months" of a car being launched, thieves can develop targeted tools to steal it, it says.

At Thatcham Research's headquarters, we are shown a device hidden within a wireless speaker that can start a car by tapping into its internal communication system. We also see a military-grade signal jammer, left behind by criminals after a theft, that can block out all electronic signals for miles around.

While manufacturers are meeting security guidelines, the government should update regulations to ensure "sophisticated [theft] countermeasures are on a vehicle as standard", says Thatcham Research's principal engineer for automotive security, Steve Launchbury.

Tech that puts a key to sleep while motionless is one option, he says, so thieves cannot hack into it. In future, it may be possible for manufacturers to remotely lock down a car reported by the owner as stolen, he adds.

The Society of Motor Manufacturers and Traders told us car makers go "above and beyond regulatory requirements", while Toyota said it "continuously enhances vehicle security".

Mike, 33, believed he had done everything he could to prevent his BMW M2 from being taken. He had CCTV, a house alarm and a protective container for his keys.

But just two months after he bought the car, two masked men broke into his home in the middle of the night and demanded the keys.

"I was kind of scared for my life," he says. Avon and Somerset Police says it is "following up all lines of enquiry", but his car has not been recovered.

Rose-Marie Clarke has been a victim of car theft twice in three years. Most recently, in March, her BMW was stolen from outside her home using an antenna to amplify the signal from the key fob inside her house. It took thieves just 35 seconds.

Clarke, like many victims we have spoken to, is critical of the police.

She says officers told her they knew the likely suspect, who had recently been released from prison, but they had been unable to prove it was him. "It's disheartening… you feel powerless because the police are powerless," she says.

Warwickshire Police said despite "extensive enquiries… an offender could not be identified". BMW UK said it worked "continuously" to improve security.

Many victims we spoke to told us they had also been hit hard by a rise in their car insurance premiums.

Across the UK, car insurance quotes have risen by 82% since 2021 and Eli Glantz, an expert in organised crime and policing at security think tank Rusi, says vehicle crime "is certainly a factor".

"As more vehicles are stolen, insurers are forced to pay out more often. Premiums are going to rise," he adds.

Vehicle crime costs the UK economy £1.7bn a year, Rusi estimates.

Since April, it has been an offence to possess electronic devices used in vehicle thefts, but Glantz believes legislation is still failing to keep pace with the criminals.

It is welcome, he says, that the government is now giving vehicle thefts more attention, but he stresses that current police resources will not meaningfully reduce the number of crimes.

Several of the police officers we spoke to privately felt similarly. They said they had confidence in their ability to take down serious, organised vehicle crime networks, provided they had the necessary level of funding and support from decision-makers.

The Home Office says it "will continue to support police" in tackling organised vehicle theft and provides £300,000 a year to help strengthen its response, mainly to the National Vehicle Crime Reduction Partnership.
