World's oldest shipwreck found off coast of Turkey

BBC News Wed, 14 Oct 2026 05:00:35 GMT

The world's oldest shipwreck has been discovered off the coast of Turkey, archaeologists say.

They have dated it to between 4,400 and 4,700 years old, which is the Early Bronze Age, and is around the same time that the Great Pyramid was being built.

The ship's wooden structure has not survived, but the ceramic storage pots it was carrying were lying on the sea floor and, remarkably, some still contain traces of what they held inside.

The archaeologists who found the ship say it was about 10m in length and was a trade vessel carrying cargo across the Mediterranean Sea.

"The shipwreck is a time capsule at the bottom of the sea," said Prof Hakan Öniz, from Akdeniz University in Turkey, who led the Mediterranean Archaeology Association expedition.

The wreck is close to the shore of Kekova Island in Antalya, Turkey. The artefacts lie scattered across an underwater slope, just 30m beneath the waves.

Three large stone anchors were found, as well as more than 30 ceramic storage pots, called amphorae.

The fact that none of the ship's wood remained meant techniques like radiocarbon dating - which measures a form of carbon in organic materials - could not be used to determine its age.

Instead, archaeologists analysed the shape of the amphorae. Because pottery styles have changed over time, different styles are linked to particular periods.

The Kekova ceramics are distinctive, with two handles, a ridge in the middle and a flat base.

Öniz said this design matched other amphorae unearthed in Egypt and the Middle East that were known to be 4,400-4,700 years old.

"Comparing the decoration techniques… we can definitely say our wreck is Early Bronze Age. This is 100%."

Divers from the expedition brought the pots up to the surface, where they are now being carefully conserved after so many thousands of years underwater.

Dr Karin Sowada, an archaeologist from Macquarie University, Australia, examined the amphorae after they were recovered. Some were intact and she was surprised to see they still contained a viscous, glossy residue.

"I looked inside and nearly fainted. I could not believe how much [residue] was visible. It wasn't just like spots…. a lot of this material was still remaining," she said.

The team is now working to find out exactly what was being carried in the amphorae.

"It could be olive oil, it could be wine, it could be tree resins," Sowada said.

"It's possible that [the residue] could actually be the result of reuse of the jars. I mean, these jars are huge - they're not things that you just ship away and then never see again. People would have emptied these jars on arrival and potentially used them for many, many, many years."

The wreck and its cargo are also shedding light on trade in the ancient world.

The archaeologists say the ship started its voyage from somewhere along the eastern coast of the Mediterranean.

They believe it was taking goods to either the ancient city of Troy, in what is now north-western Turkey, or to the Minoan people in Crete.

Travelling by sea was fraught with danger, particularly around Kekova Island, where the strong currents and winds have claimed many ships.

There are hundreds of wrecks in the area, and the Mediterranean Archaeology Association specialises in finding and studying them.

Öniz says that when he is diving on the wreck his first thoughts are about the lives of the people who were connected to this vessel.

"We are the first humans who [have] touched these objects after 5,000 years.

"We are always thinking of the sailors... the producers of the amphora and the person who carried the amphora on their shoulders to the ship, and the women who were waiting for their husbands, and they unfortunately couldn't go back."

The shipwreck is owned by the Turkish Ministry of Culture. Its minister Mehmet Nuri Ersoy said the artefacts that have been recovered would be put on display in the new Mediterranean Underwater Archaeology Museum in Antalya when it opens in the coming months.

The Kekova discovery now tops the list of the world's oldest shipwrecks.

Previously, the record holder was a wreck off the coast of Dokos, an island in southern Greece, which dates to about 4,200 years ago.

The oldest known intact shipwreck, where the wood is still preserved, was found in the Black Sea off the coast of Bulgaria. It dates to about 2,400 years ago.

Pictured: Winning entries for Wildlife Photographer of the Year 2026

BBC News Tue, 13 Oct 2026 23:26:54 GMT

An emotional scene of an elephant carrying a deceased calf has earned Italian photographer Federico Veronesi the title of Wildlife Photographer of the Year 2026.

While tracking elephants in Namibia's Huab River Valley, Veronesi spotted an unusual trail of drag marks across the sand of a dry riverbed.

Following the tracks, he came across a female elephant carrying a dead calf, its leg wedged between her tusk and trunk.

Initially, Veronesi assumed the elephant was the calf's mother. But after sharing his photographs with an elephant research project working in the area, he learned that this wasn't certain.

Researchers recognised the elephant as a female who had lost a calf several years earlier and said she had previously picked up dead calves belonging to other females.

For Veronesi, the encounter was deeply significant. "Of all the 20 years I've done photographing animals, this is the most heart-wrecking and fascinating and dramatic," he said, describing it as "revealing of the depth and complexity of the emotions, the feelings of the animals."

He hopes the image will encourage people to look beyond animals' physical appearance and recognise them as individuals with their own relationships, memories and emotions. "It's a small window that opens just a crack into the complexity of their world," he said.

The competition's annual exhibition opens at the Natural History Museum on Friday 16 October 2026. Keep scrolling to see more award-winning images.

Category: Young Grand Title and 11 to 14 Years

Photographer: Oscar Whitfield, Zimbabwe/UK

Location: Zambezi National Park, Zimbabwe

Only by lying in the dust and capturing the beetle at eye level could Whitfield show how much strength its task required.

While on a family game drive in Zimbabwe, near his home in Victoria Falls, he spotted the dung beetle struggling to push a ball of dung uphill through the sand, with a trail of flies following closely behind. After experimenting with different angles and compositions, he captured a moment that reveals the extraordinary effort involved in this seemingly simple task.

"I hope that when people look at the photograph, they can see too, and maybe appreciate how much can be happening in a small moment in the natural world," says Whitfield.

Category: 10 Years and Under

Photographer: Amelie Davis, UK

Location: Surrey, England

You do not need a camera or a plane ticket to take an award-winning photo. Davis captured this shot in the garden using her dad's smartphone.

Reform UK has been rocked by a fresh crisis after councillors and election candidates for the party were caught on film making graphic threats of extreme violence and expressing racist and misogynistic views.

Three councillors have already stood down from the party, which said it was conducting an investigation over the first revelations from an ITV undercover investigation that aired on Wednesday.

Farage has made repeated pledges over the last two years to rid Reform of “extremists” and “bigots” and introduce vetting. The party says it carries out vetting checks on everyone who wants to stand for election.

However, the first clips from ITV’s year-long undercover investigation showed Alan Feeney, a Reform councillor and deputy mayor of Solihull, calling Asian homeowners “parasites,” and stating that “no more than 1% of [the] British population can be non-white”.

Chris Whelan announced he had resigned from Reform on 12 October “on personal and health grounds”. Campbell is still in post.

Israel blocks construction of key Gaza base created by Trump peace board

The Guardian Wed, 14 Oct 2026 19:19:52 GMT

Exclusive: Israel has not permitted US-led project to house 150 international troops to move forward

Israel is refusing to allow a key military outpost project for Donald Trump’s Board of Peace to proceed in Gaza, the Guardian has learned.

In early August, the Board of Peace, the international organization authorized in 2025 by the UN and chaired by the US president, issued its first construction contract for Gaza: Louisiana-based Arkel International would construct a military outpost for 150 international troops that would be the vanguard of an international stabilization force.

Two months later, Israel has still not permitted the contract to move forward, according to a person with direct knowledge of the matter. Contractors and trucks are not being allowed to cross into Gaza and begin the work. “Israel won’t open the border,” they said.

A Board of Peace spokesperson declined to comment. The Israeli Defense Forces and the Israeli government declined to comment.

Israel’s war in Gaza is one of the eight wars Trump claims to have resolved. He listed it again last week in a screed on social media, protesting that it was unfair he had not won the Nobel peace prize.

The Board of Peace, formed in the wake of an October 2025 ceasefire in Gaza, is central to Trump’s claims to have ended the conflict.

Arkel’s planned “mission support site temporary outpost” is decidedly rudimentary: a fortified position of about 100 meters by 100 meters, at a site that was once part of the controversial and now defunct Gaza Humanitarian Foundation project, according to the person familiar with the matter. The US-backed GHF operated in Gaza from May 2025 till October 2025 to deliver aid protected by private contractors.

The base would host short rotating deployments of a well-armed force of 150 troops in Gaza, the person said. They would come from a larger component of 500 sent already from Morocco and Kosovo.

But without this contract, the source said, the rest of the Board of Peace program cannot launch. Israel’s refusal to allow Arkel, the contractor, to build the base, essentially cuts the Board of Peace off at the legs.

“You can’t get in without [an international stabilization force (ISF)]. You can’t rebuild,” the person said. “There’s no security. There’s no deliveries of construction equipment. You can’t even start putting utilities. You can’t start putting in basic life-support facilities you know because without the ISF you can’t go in.”

The person familiar with the matter said the Board of Peace believes that Israel’s government will let the project proceed after the Israeli elections, which are scheduled for 27 October.

The controversial Board of Peace has a unique structure. Trump is designated as chair for life. Trump’s son-in-law, Jared Kushner, is an executive board member, as is real estate developer Steve Witkoff, who Trump has appointed as a mediator in various international disputes.

Trump’s new press secretary faces scrutiny over claims to be a lawyer

The Guardian Wed, 14 Oct 2026 19:12:29 GMT

Katie Zacharia describes herself as a lawyer but no public record shows she has been licensed in the US states where she resided

Donald Trump’s new press secretary, Katie Zacharia, has repeatedly described herself as a lawyer. But when asked after her inaugural press briefing on Tuesday to name the state where she passed the bar exam, she replied curiously: “when I moved to China”.

No public record shows she has ever been licensed to practice law in the US states where she resided, and her answer to reporters’ questions on Tuesday only deepened scrutiny.

Zacharia wasn’t registered in 38 states, as well as DC and the Virgin Islands, where the state’s attorney list was publicly available, according to a review from the Guardian.

After her shaky and widely panned first briefing, a reporter asked Trump which state Zacharia had passed the bar exam in. The president, who had described Zacharia as an attorney when announcing her appointment, turned to her and asked: “Which state?” She replied: “when I moved to China”.

Zacharia’s law degree is real – Pepperdine University confirmed she graduated from its law school in 2011. But a degree is not a license, and no record has surfaced showing she was admitted to practice, according to the report in Popular Information.

In a later statement to the press, Zacharia said she had “practiced international transaction law, with all the appropriate credentials”. The White House did not say whether she had passed a bar exam anywhere.

Zacharia’s LinkedIn profile lists her as general counsel of Fix California, a conservative group founded by Ric Grenell, a longtime Trump ally and the special presidential envoy for special missions. Zacharia lived in California, but the state bar of California said it had no record of her being licensed in the state.

“The State Bar of California has no record of Kate Sharon Zacharia or Kate Sharon Black (or Katie in both instances) having been licensed to practice law in California,” a spokesperson for the California state bar said in a statement. Black is her maiden name.

“Due to confidentiality laws, the State Bar can only confirm whether someone passed the bar exam, not whether they took the bar exam,” it added.

No report alleges that Zacharia has been charged with or has committed an offence.

Campaign for Accountability, a non-profit watchdog organization, on Wednesday filed a complaint with the state bar of California, asking it to investigate whether Zacharia misled the public – and potentially violated state law – by describing herself as a lawyer.

“In California, calling yourself a lawyer without a license isn’t puffery; it’s a crime,” said Michelle Kuppersmith, the group’s executive director, in a statement.

California law says anyone “advertising or holding himself or herself out as practicing or entitled to practice law” who is not an active member of the state bar could be punished with a fine of up to $1,000 and up to one year in jail.

“There is a difference between having a JD and being a lawyer, you are licensed as a lawyer by saying that you took the bar,” said Richard Painter, a university of Minnesota law professor and the former chief White House ethics lawyer under the George W Bush administration. “Calling yourself an attorney … it’s a misdemeanor at least.”

Zacharia’s LinkedIn notes that she was remote while at Fix California. But Painter said that was “not relevant – you need to be a member of the bar”.

The profile also says she worked as an associate at the Beijing firm Zhong Lun from 2011 to 2013. Only Chinese citizens can be licensed as lawyers in China, according to Chinese law and legal affairs website China Justice Observer, though foreign staff can work at Chinese firms in other capacities.

The Washington Post, CNN and the independent newsletter Popular Information also found no sign of admission elsewhere, including Texas and Washington DC, where Popular Information said she had worked. The Post found no active or expired admissions under either of her names.

The press office of Gavin Newsom, California’s governor, mocked the exchange on X, asking whether the new press secretary “appears to be a Chinese lawyer”. Steven Cheung, the White House director of communications, in the meantime described her briefing performance as “tremendous” and “commanding”. Zacharia was relatively unknown when Trump announced her selection to be his next press secretary last week, after the departure of Karoline Leavitt earlier this year. In the days since, several outlets have uncovered a series of since-deleted social media posts that promote debunked or baseless conspiracy.

In a series of now-deleted social media posts on X, reviewed by the Guardian, Zacharia fawned over Tesla and Space X founder, Elon Musk.

On 22 May 2022, the new press secretary described Musk, a tech billionaire and Trump ally, as “based”. She went on to explain the term was applied “when you want to recognize someone for being themselves” and “courageous and unique or not caring what others think”.

In another post from the same year, Zacharia also responded to one user sharing a widely circulated picture of Musk and Ghislaine Maxwell, the co-conspirator of the late sex-offender Jeffrey Epstein, at the Vanity Fair Oscar party in 2014, where Zacharia wrote “oh please, he already responded to this #oldnews”.

Musk has not been charged with any wrongdoing in connection with Epstein, and has denied knowing Maxwell, claiming the photo which pictures her standing close to him is because she “photobombed” him.

In other now-deleted posts, also from the same period, Zacharia directed various complimentary messages at Musk. “#IstandwithElon,” she wrote in one. In another she praised him for renouncing his support for the Democratic party and saying he would now support Republicans. “Smartest and richest man in the world right here is saying out loud what everyone else is thinking,” Zacharia wrote.

However, last year Zacharia questioned whether Musk could have been trusted “from the beginning” while appearing as a guest on conservative commentator Buck Sexton’s podcast.

“He’s heavily invested in China. A lot of people were questioning his allegiance to begin with. Where is his allegiance completely?” she asked on the show. In late 2025, Musk and Trump had a public falling out of the president’s sweeping domestic policy legislation. They have since reconciled and Musk, according to the New York Times, is spending record sums to help Republicans retain control of the midterm elections this cycle.

The Bulwark previously reported on Zacharia’s since-deleted posts where she called on the administration to “suspend habeas corpus” – the constitutional right to challenge detention before a judge.

Meanwhile CNN also found now-deleted social media posts where Zacharia wrote and reshared comments that accused prominent political figures and Hollywood elites of pedophilia. This includes longtime Democratic operative John Podesta – who was the notable target of the debunked Pizzagate conspiracy – which alleged that several prominent Democrats were part of a child sex-trafficking ring.

Russell Brand was a ruthless, calculated and manipulative sexual predator who boasted about wanting to have sex with 5,000 women, a court has heard.

The 51-year-old comedian is accused of having an “insatiable desire” to get what he wanted and treating women as a means to an end.

Brand faces three charges of rape, three of sexual assault and one of indecent assault against six women, in allegations spanning 10 years from 1999 to 2009. He denies all the charges and has pleaded not guilty.

The fifth complainant was a fan of Brand and had gone with a friend to see the comedian perform at the Hammersmith Apollo in London in 2009. Could AI ‘change the stakes’ in the development of bioweapons?

The Guardian Wed, 14 Oct 2026 17:34:14 GMT

Experts say the technology could help terrorists and nation states with production but that significant hurdles remain

In his annual speech on threats to the nation, Sir Ken McCallum, the head of the UK’s intelligence service, MI5, warned that AI had the potential to “change the stakes” on existing and even new bioweapons. Here we look at how AI might help in the development of bioweapons and the hurdles that remain.

It depends on who wants to make them. One risk is the rogue, unskilled bad actor or terrorist group that works from a garage laboratory. AI chatbots could help them skill up, by compiling relevant information, planning experiments and troubleshooting problems. It’s likely the work would focus on using existing pathogens and toxins.

The other risk comes from well-resourced organisations and nation states that have plenty of skilled scientists, high containment laboratories and all of the equipment and reagents needed to work on bioweapons safely. AI tools could help researchers identify genetic tweaks to make existing pathogens more lethal, for example. This is the more immediate concern, according to Dr Filippa Lentzos, a reader in science and international security at King’s College London. “Here, AI does not remove the need for expertise, it amplifies expertise that is already there,” she says.

A more distant risk comes from highly skilled groups using AI to design completely new pathogens and toxins that are resistant to drugs or have no antidotes.

Last year, police in India uncovered a suspected bioterrorism plot in which a doctor and several others drew on AI chatbot queries and AI-driven search, allegedly to help them make ricin, a deadly toxin, using kitchen tools. It is unclear whether the AI’s responses helped, but other accounts suggest the tools can provide relevant and dangerous information.

The AI company Anthropic raised further concerns in September when it reported five cases of scientists in unnamed countries trying to use its Claude chatbot for what may have been dangerous work on viruses or toxins. One red flag, the company said, was that after being blocked, the scientists found workarounds.

In one case, Anthropic said researchers used the chatbot to plan experiments that would introduce mutations in a highly pathogenic bird flu virus to make it adapt and spread more easily in mammals. The work, and the other cases, could signify attempts to make pathogens more dangerous, but such studies are also done in legitimate labs to help researchers spot future pandemics and develop vaccines and drugs to treat disease.

A flurry of studies has shown how AI tools can provide potentially dangerous information on bioweapons. In a recent exercise at MIT, non-science students were asked if they could get an AI chatbot to help them unleash a pandemic. Within an hour, the chatbots listed four potential pandemic pathogens, explained how to make them from synthetic DNA, and supplied names of DNA synthesis companies that were unlikely to screen their orders. It is easier said than done, however.

In another case, the Stanford University researcher Dr David Relman described how an AI explained how to modify a pathogen to make it resistant to treatment. Another scientist revealed that an AI chatbot had explained how to use a weather balloon to release biological weapons from the sky.

The dual-use nature of biological research can make it hard to distinguish valuable work that could protect health from attempts to develop bioweapons. In 2025, scientists at Los Alamos National Laboratory trained an AI to predict how exceptionally potent neurotoxins from marine snails latch on to human cells, work that could help in the development of antidotes. But the US government became concerned when Chinese researchers drew on the work to develop their own tool to design new toxins. The Chinese researchers said the work was aimed solely at developing drugs.

In April, scientists raised further concerns when they made the first viruses designed by AI. The viruses are a particular type that only invade bacteria, and the work could lead to new medicines, but experts warned that it raised “urgent biosafety and biosecurity questions”.

Most work on bioweapons is beyond the reach of unskilled groups: as well as being highly technical, it is extremely dangerous. While AI chatbots might be prompted to give information on bioweapons, a list of instructions is not enough. “Information is only one part of the problem,” says Lentzos. “Tacit expertise, access to biological materials and equipment, experimental validation, scale-up and delivery all remain substantial barriers.”

Sonia Ben Ouagrham-Gormley, the deputy director of the biodefense programme at George Mason University in Virginia says tacit knowledge – the unwritten knowhow that an AI is unlikely to help with – has been key in the success of historical state-run bioweapons programmes.

Prof Martin Pačesa at the University of Zurich said access to material was critical. “Any graduate physicist could sketch up plans for a potentially more effective nuclear bomb, but where is he going to get a lump of uranium from? Same for bioweapons. If you have access to material and facilities capable of producing them, then AI is not going to make too much difference if you want to do harm.”

Some AI companies are taking steps to prevent people from using their tools to develop bioweapons, but the majority are not announcing what, if anything, they are doing, says Prof Tom Inglesby, director of the Johns Hopkins Center for Health Security. “We have no formal government policies in place in any country yet to protect against this kind of misuse,” he says.

He wants formal independent evaluations of new AI models before they are released, with any dangers eliminated before the models are made public. And high-risk biological data, such as information on how pathogens might spread more easily, should be restricted. Meanwhile, companies that make genetic material for researchers must screen orders to ensure they are not for the creation of novel, highly dangerous viruses. Some screening already happens, but it is largely voluntary, and can be sidestepped if researchers use AI to design new, harmful genetic sequences that companies are not checking for.

For the future of green politics, look beyond the Green party

The Guardian Wed, 14 Oct 2026 17:15:25 GMT

Environmentalists of all political hues must find the language and ideas that turn passion for climate action into impetus

The future of Green politics is under constant discussion after the Holborn and St Pancras byelection, but a much heavier clash – in meaning, symbolism and portent – happened earlier this summer, in King’s Lynn and West Norfolk. The council declared a climate emergency in 2021, along with hundreds of others, and while it was under Conservative control. It is now controlled by independents, and a Reform UK councillor, Austen Moore, moved to scrap the declaration.

It was the end of June, and the meeting had to be called off, because of the extreme heat in the King’s Lynn council chamber. A lot of people paused to grimly remark on that irony, but not Moore, who simply rescheduled the motion for the following week, where it was voted down (by 33 to seven). A local scientist and member of Extinction Rebellion liveblogged that debate, adding helpful maps, such as how much of the county was already below the annual flood level – most of it, including King’s Lynn itself. If this all sounds like a cheerful take home, numerous other Reform-led councils have had the same debate and Reform has prevailed. Their arguments can be terrifyingly illiterate. In Kent, councillor Linden Kemkaran asserted that climate change is fine because the world was warmer in Roman times, and as “carbon dioxide … is a trace gas essential for life on Earth”, to try to cut carbon emissions would itself be dangerous.

If you’re waiting for reality to knock some sense into politics, you will wait too long. There is no heatwave, no flood, no El Niño, no omnicrisis, that will divert rightwing populism from its crusade against action on climate.

The question isn’t what the Green party alone could do to create an eco-populist wave of equal volume. A party that could single-handedly represent the 68% of people who want action on climate change doesn’t exist in modern British politics. The question is, how do you turn that strength of feeling into something more concrete, so it isn’t constantly being ram-raided by unshameable, unscientific chancers? Environmentalists in every party, leaders of every party (apart from the Conservatives, who seem hellbent on chasing Reform, despite the stated values of their supporters), need to find the language and ideas that turn passion for the environment into impetus.

In their recent report, Caroline Lucas and the green philosopher Rupert Read make the case for “eco-popularism” rather than eco-populism: a way of thinking that doesn’t try to hotwire its way into people’s emotional circuitry with invigorating binaries, but, rather, describes actions that would genuinely change lives for the better, and therefore be popular.

Rightwing populists have a set of arguments against climate action that are taking hold more generally and are self-fulfilling: the more they’re repeated, the more they win at local level, the more people come to believe that these views are generally held. Those narratives are that climate action of any sort – be it ultra-low emission zones, 15-minute cities or, most importantly, net zero – is too expensive at this time; that net zero itself is a luxury belief of the comfortable elite who don’t care about ordinary people’s living conditions (Lucas and Read paraphrase from a Nigel Farage column last year).

This so-called “Manichean” framing, the noble masses betrayed by a corrupt elite, is dog obvious, but to point to Farage and say “you, the guy in receipt of £5m from a non-domiciled crypto billionaire, you’re representing the masses against the elites?” is tempting but fruitless. No case for climate action will take hold unless it addresses the conditions that populists exploit – the cost of living, of course, but also the sense of precariousness and active fear of the future that climate events intensify.

A prime minister freezing bus fares or taking the VAT off heating doesn’t address those brute realities and deep anxieties: if anything, it shores up a sense that central government is no longer capable of building the resilience and adaptation a chaotic world demands. People need to know there’s a plan for cheaper, greener energy, food security and extreme weather events. Local, tangible actions should be supported, broadcast and celebrated, in political debate and in the media; a discussion held in abstract terms of global patterns and borderless looming disaster doesn’t just foster despair. It plays back into the hands of populists, whose incredibly proximal enemies – small boats, asylum seekers – and standing street armies create the opposite impression, of agency and might. It doesn’t matter that it’s specious and vindictive – it matters that it lands and there is no answering call to arms. One of the many paradoxes of the climate debate is that the far right talks as though disaster is not real, but acts as though it’s imminent. The left does the opposite, talking of catastrophe but acting as if there’s time to lose.

Without a change of direction, even those of us who are constitutionally not susceptible to rightwing populism will still give in to its echo, which is hopelessness. Coherence is desperately needed: to promise defence spending at the expense of heat pumps, or high-quality jobs with the downside of new fossil-fuel exploration, is simply to reinforce a sense of impotence, nationally and individually.

Sign up to Matters of Opinion

Guardian columnists and writers on what they’ve been debating, thinking about, reading, and more

after newsletter promotion

King’s Lynn and West Norfolk’s debate in July had an interesting pivot from Reform: they had started to magpie the language of “resilience building”, in place of climate action. But they didn’t mean it to describe peat and wildlife restoration, or food cooperatives, or retrofitted housing, or community-owned wind power. They weren’t interested in solar panels or sustainable drainage or civic infrastructure. It was the broadest possible false promise: spend less on climate, therefore less has been spent, therefore your community is more resilient. Councillors from all the other parties united to defeat them, and politics national and local can learn from that unity; but no party can afford to wait for an opponent to unite against.

Zoe Williams is a Guardian columnist

Manchester City staff invited on government trade trip to India

The Guardian Wed, 14 Oct 2026 17:00:08 GMT

Exclusive: Greater Manchester mayor Bev Craig invited City representative on trade trip organised by business secretary

A club representative from Manchester City was invited on a government-backed trade delegation to India this week, despite the club being found guilty of 114 breaches of Premier League rules.

Andy Burnham’s successor as Manchester mayor Bev Craig took a representative from the club to Delhi and Mumbai on a trip organised by business secretary Jonathan Reynolds, where she and other northern mayors have been promoting British trade and tourism.

She defended the decision to invite City even as the prime minister came under fire for his own contacts with the club, telling the Guardian it had played a vital role in helping build infrastructure in her city.

“If a football club has broken the rules, a football club should be punished for breaking the rules, and that is pretty clear in an independent process,” she said.

“I think what can also be true at the same time though, if you ask residents in east Manchester, have they benefited from a new library, a new leisure centre, a new sixth-form college, infrastructure investment, the job opportunities that come from them being a good employment charter signee and an early adopter of the real living wage – then they will have felt that benefit.”

City, which says it is innocent of the charges and has appealed against them, declined to comment.

The revelation comes as Burnham faced pressure over his relationship with City’s owners during his time as mayor of Greater Manchester. The prime minister was forced to clarify on Wednesday that he had two additional dinners with the club chair, Khaldoon al-Mubarak, at the Etihad Stadium, having previously declared those only as matchday hospitality.

Speaking at PMQs, Burnham said no one from the club had raised the Premier League charges with him.

Craig’s defence of the club came as her mayoral authority published details of Burnham’s contacts with City officials when he was mayor.

The release showed the prime minister had attended a formal meeting with al-Mubarak, chatted to him in the stands at a player’s testimonial game and was accompanied by staff from the club on official overseas visits.

But the Sun newspaper revealed later on Monday that Burnham had twice attended a pre-match dinner with the chair, which was declared in now-deleted council paperwork. No 10 said it was routine for the records to be deleted when a new mayor was in place.

Burnham’s initial response to the findings against City had drawn criticism after he said he would be “really concerned” if its Abu Dhabi owners sold the club in the wake of the Premier League findings, calling them a “huge partner in the building of modern Manchester”.

As the prime minister came under fire for his comments, Downing Street clarified that he believed where wrongdoing had been established, those responsible should face the consequences.

City attended the trade delegation alongside several other northern sports and leisure organisations, including Lancashire cricket club and Visit Lake District. While in India, the football club took part in an event in Delhi promoting British tourism.

The Great North – a coalition of mayors from the north of England which organised the India event – said its aim was “to shine a spotlight on the great tourism products across the north of England”.​

The eyes have it: Reform politician’s ‘smart glasses’ prompt calls for ban in Westminster

The Guardian Wed, 14 Oct 2026 16:43:27 GMT

Jason O’Connell’s suspected use of the specs to aid his speech in Senedd reignites debate over AI-enabled devices

It was a flowing speech packed full of precise statistics – but Jason O’Connell, a Reform UK Senedd member, was not quite the gifted orator he had first appeared. The periodic flashing in his spectacles seemed to give the game away as he spoke for five minutes in the Senedd chamber without a glance at his notes.

“Cardiff reviewed 192 roads, following 933 requests, and only four routes have returned to 30mph,” he said as he led the party’s charge to abolish 20mph traffic zones. “£38.3m wasted to implement this policy and up to £8.9bn in an act of economic self-harm.”

It was probably no feat of memory – rather, the Reform member for Pontypridd Cynon Merthyr was suspected of using smart glasses that turned his lenses into tiny teleprompters.

In the days afterwards, Senedd members said the issue of smart glasses – popularised by Mark Zuckerberg’s Meta – needed to be dealt with at pace, with explicit guidance developed on their use.

There was no suggestion that O’Connell’s glasses were recording. But it has prompted a fresh debate about the increasingly popular technology, with the House of Commons facing calls to ban smart glasses at Westminster.

The Liberal Democrats wrote to Lindsay Hoyle, the Commons speaker, on Wednesday urging him to act amid the “increasing use of smart glasses” on the parliamentary estate. They warned that with Christmas coming, even more people could start using the AI-enabled devices in 2027.

The technology has myriad implications. Anna Sabine, the Liberal Democrat spokesperson for culture, media and sport, said smart glasses could lead to debates becoming increasingly “managed”.

“How much is the quality of what an MP is saying actually down to what they’re getting through the AI glasses?” she said. “It’s a really interesting question.”

She said she had not yet identified anyone wearing smart glasses in parliament but added: “This is the problem: I wouldn’t necessarily know.” A law would help to keep the estate and the work MPs did “private and secure”.

Sabine added: “Smart glasses can record video and take photographs discreetly … creating a particular risk to the existing rules on the parliamentary estate [limiting recording and photography].

“I am regularly in meetings with documents or talking about important, private matters on behalf of my constituents. I am sure you will agree that the details of such cases should never be at risk of being shared because someone is filming or taking pictures discreetly.

“Because smart glasses look like ordinary glasses, and increasingly their recording indicator lights can be covered or disabled, filming could easily go unnoticed by security teams, putting private information at risk.”

The Guardian Wed, 14 Oct 2026 16:05:22 GMT

Palantir and the London mayor, Sadiq Khan, have called off their legal fight over claims the Labour mayor was blocking the US tech company from signing a contract with the Metropolitan police because of its values and ethics.

Palantir and the mayor’s office for policing and crime (Mopac) announced on Wednesday they had withdrawn allegations against each other, heading off a high court trial scheduled for January.

Palantir sued Mopac in June after Khan stepped in to block it from awarding Palantir a two-year, £50m deal for Scotland Yard to use AI to process intelligence in criminal investigations in a system called unified operational analytics (UOA), citing a “clear and serious breach” of procurement rules.

With Palantir also working for the Israeli military and for the Trump administration in its immigration crackdown, Khan has previously said Londoners only wanted to see public money being paid to companies that “share the values of our city”.

Louis Mosley, who heads Palantir in the UK and Europe, responded by accusing Khan of politicising procurement and said he was “putting politics above public safety”. He said: “What Londoners value is not being mugged, not being raped by a serving police officer.”

The agreement to drop the claims against each other comes a month after Palantir hired Tom Watson, the former Labour party deputy leader, as a full-time lobbyist. The firm was concerned that if public authorities were able to block it from winning contracts due to objections over the values of its senior leadership, it would undermine its ability to operate in the UK.

Palantir is facing a battle to retain its £330m NHS medical data contract amid a vociferous campaign from parts of the public and some MPs for it to be replaced by a less controversial company.

Palantir and Mopac said in a joint statement that Mopac’s decision in May to block the contract “was not based on Palantir’s values or ethics or any wider reporting about the company”.

“Mopac recognises Palantir’s concerns that media reporting of a statement from the mayor’s spokesperson before the decision was taken could have incorrectly created the impression that an assessment of the company’s values had been a factor in that decision,” it said.

“Mopac’s consideration was based upon its statutory role of examining spend proposals, against the appropriate legal tests and the relevant guidance on procurement, governance, affordability and ensuring value for money for Londoners.”

Khan said: “London is the technology capital of Europe, and I am proud that leading software firms choose the city as their home. Palantir makes a significant contribution to jobs and investment in the capital, employing a large number of Londoners whose services have been procured legally by organisations across the UK and beyond.”

‘Try to eat really well, 80% of the time’: nutrition expert says healthy eating is easier than you think

The Guardian Wed, 14 Oct 2026 16:00:06 GMT

Christopher Gardner, the author of Food Sense and director of nutrition studies at Stanford, says we have overcomplicated the question of what to eat

Finally, some good news: eating well is more straightforward than you think. “There’s a lot of good science out there, and it mostly agrees,” says Stanford University’s director of nutrition studies and professor of medicine Christopher Gardner from his office in Palo Alto – beaming, bronzed and the picture of health.

In the age of social media and wellness grift, we’ve overcomplicated the question of what to eat, he says.

In 2023, Gardner was invited to serve on the US government’s dietary guidelines advisory committee, reviewing the official guidelines for healthy eating and recommending updates. “It was quite an honour to be asked,” remembers Gardner. It was also “a tonne of work”.

Gardner was more than up for the task. He has researched nutrition for more than 30 years, regularly advising official bodies on the latest science and conducting his own gold-standard studies on everything from fermented foods to fish oil.

But when, after two years of work, the committee submitted its 400-page report, it was summarily dismissed by Robert F Kennedy Jr, the Trump administration’s new health secretary, and his team.

Now Gardner’s view is that expert advice is more necessary than ever for navigating the noise around nutrition. His new book, Food Sense, brings together everything he’s learned to give straightforward, science-backed advice on “what to eat and why”.

In a surprisingly optimistic interview, Gardener spoke about what’s being missed in the binary of “miracle foods” and “ultra-processed” ones, the politicisation of nutrition science and how science can help us to make better choices at every meal.

Answers have been condensed for sense and clarity.

Why did you believe this book was needed?

I give a lot of talks and, afterwards, people have often said to me: “Thank you for clarifying, and not talking down to me, and being practical, and acknowledging that I have a limited budget or access to healthy food.” Everyone eats, but there’s so much fearmongering and bickering about what to eat – I thought I could find a way to pull a bunch of this information together, and give everybody a better chance to do their best.

Why is there so much debate over how to eat?

For a start, there are different kinds of science, and it’s never complete. I’ve done 25 human intervention trials, always trying to answer practical questions. For example, we did a study testing whether real garlic or garlic supplements can lower the bad LDL [low-density lipoprotein] cholesterol. The public’s interest, of course, is: “Is garlic good for you?”, but you can’t design a study that answers such a general question. We have to look at a particular dose, population, duration and outcome.

As I advanced in my career, I started being invited to serve on nutritionary advisory committees, such as for the American Heart Association and the American Diabetes Association. Reviewing papers with other scientists, we almost always found that we agreed: even if we didn’t have all the data we needed, we had enough to say that people should probably eat this or that. What I kept noticing, again and again, was that it really wasn’t hard to reach consensus.

If there’s so much agreement about what we should be eating, why does the advice seem so contradictory?

We’ve all seen the headlines saying that “eggs are good for you” – then they’re bad, then they’re good again. If you’d followed the science, it’s more that we thought this one thing about eggs, then we did more studies and found there was more to the story, and, since then, the advice has been pretty consistent – but it’s hard to attract attention with that. If you want to make clickbait, you can say something quite plausible that’s not scientifically accurate.

I do feel sorry for people with this information overload. They just think, “I give up – I’m going to eat this inexpensive, convenient thing, and to hell with it.” The food industry has done such a good job of passing on responsibility: “We just make what people are asking for, and shame on you if you can’t choose properly.” But how can you choose properly in this context?

Your advice in your book is consistent with author Michael Pollan’s famous 2006 dictum: “Eat food. Not too much. Mostly plants.” Why has that message failed to land?

I love bringing up that quote. I jokingly complain about it all the time: how many citations does he have? None! As a scientist, having to back up everything I say with evidence, I could never get away with that. But when I’m asked to summarise all of the nutrition I have studied and researched, that 20-year-old phrase of seven words really does capture most of what I have found.

We’re definitely overcomplicating it. The interest in the microbiome is a perfect example: you can now find out the particular microbes in your gut, in theory allowing you to “personalise your diet”. The problem is, there are hundreds of gut microbes, and we have not perfectly classified them all as healthy versus pathogenic.

Precision medicine is a really interesting idea, and a goal, but the commercial industry is getting ahead of us: there are now people eating a lot of junk food, then having a “gut shot”. That could help, but you’d be better off eating some veggies, fruit and legumes, and getting that solid foundation that all health professionals agree on first.

What do you make of ultra-processed foods, the latest category to be demonised?

In the US, 60% of the average diet is now classified as “ultra-processed”.

I think Carlos Monteiro, the Brazilian nutritional scientist who coined the term, made a brilliant observation, looking beyond added sugar, added salt, refined grains and saturated fats to the other cosmetic additives in those junk foods; but I think he made a poor choice with the phrasing, “ultra-processed”.

If you think about the transition from groats – that’s the hulled oat kernel – to steel-cut oats, to rolled oats, to quick-cooking rolled oats, to oat flour: they are different levels of processing, but each one is still basically just oats. Dr Monteiro was mostly concerned with the cosmetic additives: the artificial flavours or colours, the emulsifiers, the gelling, the foaming – all the things the industry does to food that is like putting lipstick on a pig.

I think, as [former FDA commissioner] Dr David Kessler said, that this is more about the formulation than it is the processing. Even breads or tomato sauces can be demonised as “ultra-processed”, because they have at least one of those additives. But health professionals are more worried about things like cookies’n’cream “high protein” cereals. Those are already junk foods; I’m not sure that additionally demonising them as “ultra-processed” helps consumers.

You write that there’s no such thing as a super-food, and even dark chocolate should be considered candy and an occasional treat. What’s one rule that does work for making better overall choices?

Try to eat really well, 80% of the time. You’re much more likely to stick with a healthy diet that has room for a few exceptions than if you hold yourself to being exclusive and restrictive. Beyond that, it’s about treating every meal as an opportunity to make good choices. Let’s say you’re the average American, eating sugary breakfast cereals. Immediately, as a nutritionist, I’m going to say eggs are better – but if you’re having eggs with cheese, sausage and bacon, that’s just a saturated fats nightmare.

Sign up to Well Actually

Practical advice, expert insights and answers to your questions about how to live a good life

after newsletter promotion

Another example is coffee. In 2015, it was adopted into the US dietary guidelines as being consistent with better health, even at three to five cups a day. Most people don’t know that. But it’s not mocha-frappa-crappaccino – it’s black coffee, or with small amounts of sweetener and dairy.

What have you found to be effective in changing people’s eating habits?

The science alone often isn’t enough to convince people to change their eating behaviours, but eating in a way that aligns with your personal values can help, and often it happens to line up with health.

If I’m given a choice between a steak or a salad, even if I’m aspiring to eat better, it’s easy to say “I can start tomorrow,” because the only person I’m hurting by choosing the steak is me. But if I understand more about food, I know that my choices are affecting others. It’s not just about cholesterol levels: there are impacts for the person working in a slaughterhouse in dangerous conditions; the production of CO2, and climate change; and the inhumane slaughter of animals.

There’s more to nutrition than knowing about the biochemical pathways. If you engage with animal rights, human labour issues, the environment and all the different things that food is connected to, it can be easier to make the behaviour change and maintain it: “I did this for a reason, and I can keep doing it.”

Your 2023 study, comparing vegan and omnivore diets in 22 pairs of identical twins, showed wide-ranging, conclusive benefits for the vegan group. Does that mean, in an ideal world, we would all be vegans?

Not necessarily. French fries, Oreos and soda are all vegan, but not nutritious. Also, the term “vegan” can be very polarising. The alternative term “plant-based” is now being used instead, including for packaged foods that aren’t necessarily healthy for you.

I’ve lately transitioned to recommending a “whole-food, plant-rich” diet, which I hope will be interpreted as rich in flavour and variety but not exclusively plants, or necessarily vegan. If people would just eat more plants, that would address most issues.

Ten years ago, the Culinary Institute of America came up with this concept of the “protein flip”, making vegetables, grains and beans the center of the plate, then adding a little bit of meat: a couple of strips of chicken, not a whole breast. That gets rid of the polarisation, of vegans versus carnivores, and even brings people together. You’d have a bigger global impact getting omnivores to eat half as much meat than you would from doubling the number of vegans.

Tell me about your time on the 2025 dietary guidelines advisory committee. How did you feel when your report was dismissed?

Twenty of us volunteered for 10 hours a week, for two years, and produced a 400-page report and a 1,000-page supplementary report. We handed it in in December 2024, just as the administration was handed over to Trump; almost immediately, after Robert F Kennedy Jr took over, he said: “I’m not using any of this.” We were devastated. Like, you’ve got to be kidding me – we had each put in 1,000 hours! We were well aware that we were an advisory committee, and that the government rarely takes all of the advice, but, in more than 20 years, it’s never been completely dismissed.

Personally, I have been very vocal: we were slandered. The government said we had “conflicts of interest”. Technically, that is true: among the 20 of us, there was a long list – for example, one of my 25 studies was funded by Beyond Meat – but many were very benign, and decades ago, all of them had been reported and made public before we did our work. In the committee that replaced us, five of the nine advisers had direct ties to meat and dairy, and stronger conflicts. The hypocrisy was just mind-boggling.

Even more heinous, in my opinion, was that they dismissed our report for using a “health equity” lens, which they said was not appropriate for science. We were tasked with doing that: these were the dietary guidelines for all Americans, not white Americans, so we tried to address any possible differences related to race and ethnicity, or socioeconomic position. Because of the limited available data, I remember making only one substantive change on that basis, and it had to do with dairy consumption for non-white people. We didn’t demonise dairy, though I know it pissed off the industry; we simply changed the wording to suggest it wasn’t necessary to a healthy diet, to acknowledge the differences for racial groups. My experience of the government was that there was repression of science, racism and hypocrisy.

What do you make of the guidelines ultimately produced by Kennedy’s replacement committee?

Most of them were fine. They do include recommendations based on solid science, many carried forward from the past: don’t eat too many calories; have veggies, fruit and whole grains; limit added sugar. But in our report, the most consistent finding was to replace red and processed red meat with beans, peas and lentils. The new committee replaced that with advice to prioritise protein at every meal, including red meat, and put a steak alongside the food pyramid. It was a slap in the face.

But the difficulties go beyond politics – it’s the food industry, lobbying, marketing, social media, all causing more confusion. Although I wish there was less misinformation, it means I have job security forever.

With so many structural barriers to healthy eating, is there any hope for widespread change?

I’ve been convinced that people aren’t widely going to go back to scratch-cooking, so I’ve shifted my research to institutional food settings. I think I can have the biggest impact by trying to work with chefs on making the default choices healthier, and improving the menus offered by universities, hospitals, senior centres, prisons and the military.

We’re going to see how this plays out over the next decade in schools. The current administration has instructed schools to get rid of all UPFs [ultra-processed foods], but schools will say: “You don’t give us enough money for real food.” The government will have to force the industry to make better options and charge reasonable prices; give the schools more money; or, hopefully, both. It costs a lot more to eat well, and it takes more time, but the obvious answer is that our kids are worth it.

I’m actually pretty optimistic. The food environment is so obscene, in so many places, that there is an emerging counter-movement, which I hope my book contributes to.

Food Sense: What to Eat and Why by Christopher Gardner is out now in the US and published in the UK on 15 October

Jason Wilcox: ‘best eyes’ at Manchester United or promoted too far and too fast?

The Guardian Wed, 14 Oct 2026 15:42:11 GMT

Director of football is tarnished by recruitment record and Amorim stint but has received glowing review from above

Lisandro Martínez is not naive. At 28, he has experienced plenty in football and knows the causes of success and failure. He has a World Cup winner’s medal, whereas Jason Wilcox has overseen the latest period of mediocrity at Manchester United, during which the focus has fallen more on the team’s performances than the wider reasons behind it.

Martínez was not solely speaking for himself when, after United’s defeat at Atlético Madrid, he strongly criticised Wilcox. His views are shared by others at United frustrated by the output from above and who “expect” more from a director of football. Wilcox knows he is under pressure because the inadequate results cannot all be pinned on Michael Carrick, who is working with a squad unable to cope with the rigours of challenging at the top of the Premier League and facing Europe’s elite.

Clubs live and die by their recruitment, and United sitting 13th in the Premier League after one win from six matches does not suggest the squad is in rude health. A sporting director, like a referee, goes unnoticed when performing well but it has reached the point at United where players can take no more.

Three years at Ajax taught Martínez about football structure. The Dutch clubs mix homegrown players with imports from around the world, many of whom are sold on for large profits. Wilcox would struggle to make the case that any of his recruits, with the possible exception of Senne Lammens, have markedly increased in value. There are many sporting directors more adept at finding undervalued talent. Wilcox was offered the chance to sign Elliot Anderson from Nottingham Forest a year before the midfielder signed for Manchester City, but decided against moving for a player who continued to grow in value and was eventually out of United’s price range. If Sir Jim Ratcliffe is so eager to run United as a business, questioning why the outlay on players leads to so little return would be a sensible starting point.

Wilcox arrived at Old Trafford in April 2024 after less than a year as the director of football in the Championship with Southampton, who gave him his first executive role in senior football. Before that, the former Blackburn and Leeds winger had worked in youth development, eventually becoming Manchester City’s academy director, and was well respected, but whether this was sufficient grounding for leading United’s football operation was debatable. Wilcox oversaw at City the emergence of a glut of talent, including Phil Foden, Cole Palmer and Morgan Rogers. Some of those who have dealt with him feel a senior academy position is where his skills lie.

A key reason why he was brought back to his native north-west by United was Omar Berrada’s appointment as chief executive. The pair worked together at City and there was a high degree of mutual respect, supported by the – now tainted – achievements of the club. Wilcox was given the title technical director and was supposed to work under the experienced sporting director Dan Ashworth, who held similar roles at Brighton and Newcastle but lasted only five months under Ratcliffe, earning Wilcox a rapid promotion.

Some United staff in place when Wilcox arrived grew frustrated at the number of former City Football Group appointments being made. United had got plenty wrong over the years but continued to thrive in certain aspects, so trying to turn the club into a copy of one of their fiercest rivals grated.

Wilcox was influential in the recruitment of Ruben Amorim, who had a specific way of playing but not the players to carry out his demands. The experiment failed, accelerated by a breakdown in relationship between the head coach and Wilcox, who did not see eye-to-eye on tactics, among other things.

When it comes to players there is increasingly a focus on Premier League experience with Wilcox; this summer Youri Tielemans, Andrey Santos and Carlos Baleba arrived from top-flight English clubs for a combined £155m. The midfield did require an overhaul, with Casemiro leaving and Manuel Ugarte, currently injured, never reaching the requisite level. That outlay, however, meant there was no money for a left-back or backup striker, leaving fans eager for an explanation. In the defeat at Atlético, Carrick played the 20-year-old centre-back Ayden Heaven out of position to cover Luke Shaw’s latest absence, summing up the shallowness of the squad.

Sign up to Football Daily

Kick off your evenings with the Guardian's take on the world of football

after newsletter promotion

Ratcliffe, the minority owner, is on record as saying he has a lot of time for Wilcox, “When I listen to Jason speak, I get what he’s saying,” he told the Times in March 2025. “He’s a warm character, he’s got a sense of humour, he knows what he’s talking about.” At matches, the pair can be often found sitting next to one another in discussion. Ratcliffe has displayed a ruthless business approach since investing in United, but removing Wilcox would be one of the more difficult personal decisions. Their relationship is strong and making a change would be bold. With finances likely to be tight in January, the planning at this stage is for next summer and who is best to navigate it.

If results continue to be substandard, Ratcliffe and Berrada will be forced to consider their options. Wilcox’s bond with the pair is important but Carrick has a lot more credit in the bank with a fanbase that wields power and has strong reservations over the director of football. Removing Wilcox would be the more widely popular decision but also an acceptance of failure from the top brass. Ratcliffe once said Wilcox had the “best eyes in the club” but he cannot be blinded by the truth.

In Germany the mood is dark. But the Weimar narrative is a far right trap

The Guardian Wed, 14 Oct 2026 04:00:45 GMT

The final season of the TV epic Babylon Berlin is taking us back to 1933. Our confidence is falling apart, but that’s where the parallels end

There is one experience we in Germany can all find mildly satisfying this autumn, as we watch the dark and terrifying final season of the hit TV series Babylon Berlin. We know everything those fearless (a few of them) or frightened (many more) and opportunistic (the majority) people on the screen do not. We know their future.

Made by the public broadcaster ARD, Babylon Berlin has run for over 10 years, brilliantly fictionalising how Germany sleepwalked into nazism and the second world war. The fifth and last season begins in January 1933 with Adolf Hitler appointed Reichskanzler.

Our funny and beautiful police detective superheroine Charlotte Ritter and the also beautiful, less funny and slightly more dubious detective Gereon Rath – both fictional – are still arguing and falling in and out of love as they chase criminals and Nazis, who are becoming synonymous. Berlin is still singing, dancing. loving. Newspapers are still being printed. Trains still run. The Jewish lawyer Hans Litten – a historical figure – still believes in German laws and German integrity (the real Litten ended up killing himself in Dachau in 1938).

Watching the show 93 years after the events being depicted, I constantly want to shout at the screen: turn left here – you can kill Hitler at this dinner, and the Reichstag won’t burn, and it will all end differently. Wake up, I want to scream at the opportunistic majority – don’t you understand what is happening?

They don’t, of course. They turn the other way and fail to kill Hitler. Because facts are facts. The dramatists are accurately describing February 1933 as the month in which German society was radically torn apart at an extraordinary speed because of mistakes made by party leaders and others who could still have stopped it all. ⁠

The timing could hardly be more apt. Babylon Berlin began streaming in 2017, the year the extreme-right Alternative für Deutschland (AfD) entered the Bundestag for the first time with 12.6 % of the vote. Nine years on, the AfD has become my country’s dominant political force. In September it won 44% of the vote in state elections in Saxony-Anhalt and 38.3% in Mecklenburg-Western Pomerania. In the latter contest, Chancellor Friedrich Merz’s CDU dropped to 4.9% and didn’t even garner enough votes to enter the state parliament – an unprecedented humiliation.

It is, therefore, quite tempting to watch Babylon Berlin and see ourselves in those historic times. But does it make sense? It does, but not in the way you might expect.

There are not millions of desperate unemployed people on today’s German streets, nor paramilitary armies fighting heated battles. There is no Versailles trauma and the last global financial crisis was in 2008. There is no German president systematically replacing parliamentary government with emergency rule.

The German men’s football team coach, Jürgen Klopp, recently advised the nation to look at its strengths rather than constantly stare at what is not working, so let’s start there. Germany has a strong constitutional court system and powerful federal states; it has, with help from its partners, created and maintained European integration, along with an established democratic culture and institutions designed explicitly because of the lessons of Weimar. Our European Germany in 2026 is not Weimar Germany in 1933.

But there is another connection between Babylon Berlin and us as German spectators today – an echo that is more subtle and more interesting. It lies in this conviction that everything is falling apart. Here the wonderfully tongue-breaking term selbsterfüllende Prophezeiung (self-fulfilling prophecy) comes into play.

Germany excels at the one thing no country should aspire to be good at. We are constantly describing ourselves as a country in terminal decline. Just spend an evening watching political talkshows or listening to people around you, and you will conclude that we are witnessing the final collapse of a failed state.

The trains are all late. Bridges and streets are crumbling. Schools are failing and German children lagging behind in Pisa rankings. Bureaucracy is suffocating business and taxes are sky-high, plus ineffectively used. Isn’t energy too expensive and our pension system unsustainable? Immigration is out of control. Our digital transformation is a joke. And as for our car industry, that is on its knees. Plus, we lost to Ecuador and Paraguay at the World Cup. And the government cannot agree on how to fix any of this because support for the two big mainstream parties (the centre-right CDU and the centre-left SPD) is shrinking, and coalitions between parties that hate each other have become ever more complicated and fragile.

Some of this is true – painfully so.

I started writing these lines on a train from Hamburg to Tübingen via Stuttgart which was delayed by two hours. Someone in the hierarchy of Deutsche Bahn (or an AI, of course) then decided that, as we were already this late, the train would terminate in Mannheim. I had to cancel my plans and instead turn around and go back home. This is how you spend a day in modern Germany.

Something much larger than my individual problem has arisen, however: our national narrative of failure has developed a disturbing relationship with reality. Germany’s seasonally adjusted unemployment rate climbed back above 3 million in September and inflation reached 3.3%.

Sign up to This is Europe

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

after newsletter promotion

Within the world of public broadcasting, talk about reform has been imminent for more than a decade – but instead of building the strongest and most robust system possible, which then might actually withstand the shocks, there are simply more meetings, and more reports, and more commissions of inquiry.

The biggest problem, though, is Germany’s belief in the enormity of its problems. And this is exactly where Babylon Berlin and its look at 1933 becomes a more relevant companion to 2026 than the “Weimar is back” cry suggests .

Democracies become vulnerable when people cease to believe that democracy can deliver. The destruction of Weimar was preceded by such a loss of faith. The circumstances were and are fundamentally different now. But one political mechanism is recognisable: the extreme right has a brief and simple story to tell.

Close the borders. Deport those people we don’t know. Ignore Brussels. Stop paying Ukraine. Bring back cheap Russian energy and abolish stupid climate rules. Put Germany first.

Our society seems scared and exhausted from years of crisis after crisis, and from the alarming talk of never-ending crises of tomorrow. The AfD understands this and knows how to turn every minor and unresolved problem into evidence that “das System” is rotten. My cancelled train is no longer just that, but proof of national doom. The brilliance of declinism lies in the fact that it is self-confirming. Once you have accepted that the country is collapsing, you see nothing but evidence for it.

Would we recognise the end of our democracy if it were happening today, and to us? Or, instead, would we recognise the strength of our democracy if it were not ending?

There is a danger in catastrophising so much that we cease to notice resilience, and progress, and that many things work just fine. German angst becomes German cynicism; cynicism turns to contempt; and, finally, contempt will make the AfD’s promise of destruction sound like the renewal we all need.

We can and must tell the other, true story, of a country that has found trusted friends and partners and hence a peace that has lasted for 80 years, and economic and political, cultural and scientific successes most nations dream of – even in football (although it has become increasingly difficult to remember those days).

This is not 1933 revisited, but it is a serious moment. And a good moment to for a reminder that, in dangerous times, a democratic coalition needs to govern as decisively, effectively and empathetically as it possibly can.

Klaus Brinkbäumer, a former executive editor at Der Spiegel, is a podcast and TV host in Germany and has published nine books

Ukraine’s Vulnerability Grows as Starlink Is Restricted and Russia Escalates

The New York Times October 14, 2026

Russia-Ukraine War

Advertisement

Supported by

A huge attack cut power to 600,000 people in Kyiv, and a Pentagon-funded satellite internet program appeared to be curbed without warning.

By Constant Méheut

Reporting from Kyiv, Ukraine

Nearly five years into its fight for national survival, Ukraine has entered a moment of intense vulnerability, under growing pressure not only from Russia but also now from the United States.

A double blow came overnight on Wednesday.

Ukraine said that its military, with no warning, had lost some critical access to a military version of Starlink, a pillar of its battlefield communications. Kyiv relies on the satellite internet network, known as Starshield, which is paid for by the Pentagon. That led Ukrainian officials and analysts to surmise that the restrictions had come from Washington, as President Trump is locked in his latest feud with Ukraine’s leadership.

At the same time, Russia unleashed dozens of ballistic missiles in a broad attack on Ukraine’s energy infrastructure early Wednesday, cutting power to 600,000 people in the capital, Kyiv. It was a dark omen for a rapidly approaching winter in which Russia is gearing up to try to push Ukraine to the breaking point as Kyiv runs low on lifesaving air defenses.

It was unclear how long the Starshield restrictions would remain in place. Still, the Trump administration’s treatment of Ukraine has caused outrage in a country already reeling from a string of challenges.

Its energy system is at risk of collapse in the coming months as Russia pounds power plants and substations, trying to deprive Ukrainians of heat and water. Its economy is being pushed to the brink by Russian strikes on its ports, warehouses and steel factories. And its budget faces a multibillion-dollar gap this year and next that could leave its military severely underfunded.

The Starshield development was interpreted in Ukraine as part of a Trump administration pressure campaign to limit Ukraine’s ability to fight. Mr. Trump is pushing Kyiv to stop attacking oil refineries so that Russia can pump more diesel onto global markets as high prices threaten the prospects of Mr. Trump’s Republican Party in the midterm elections.

“Clearly, this action cannot be a coincidence and is linked to the current political situation,” Serhiy Beskrestnov, a Ukrainian military expert who advises President Volodymyr Zelensky on weapons technologies, wrote on social media about the Starlink restrictions.

SpaceX, which operates Starlink and Starshield, and its chief executive, Elon Musk, did not respond to requests for comment. The Pentagon did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Ukraine was stunned to discover the Starshield restrictions, which it said caused disruptions to several combat operations. The Ukrainian military uses Starshield in part to guide its ever-growing fleets of drones, including interceptors used to down Russian drones. The restrictions, Ukrainian officials said, tied Ukraine’s hands while leaving Russia free to wage war.

Perhaps nowhere was that imbalance clearer than in the Russian air assault overnight. The barrage included 39 ballistic missiles, Mr. Zelensky said. That would be the largest such salvo during the entire war, according to a New York Times data set based on Ukrainian Air Force figures.

Even as Russia pounds Ukraine day after day, leading to some of the highest civilian death tolls seen in years of war, Kyiv says it is abiding by Mr. Trump’s demand not to strike Russian oil refineries. Its campaign against refineries, which had led to fuel shortages across Russia, made Russians feel the war as never before.

Kyiv has said it is unfair that Russia is still attacking Ukraine’s power plants and substations. Mr. Zelensky has asserted that the strikes violate Mr. Trump’s self-declared energy cease-fire in Ukraine. The Trump administration views the cease-fire as covering only strikes on oil refineries, even though Ukraine has none that are operational.

Ukraine has set aside its most potent tool against Russia in part because it is worried that Mr. Trump could follow through on an apparent threat to stop providing intelligence that Kyiv needs to warn its people of incoming Russian strikes.

While Ukraine’s European partners have so far stepped in to patch Ukraine’s ballooning budget gaps as Russia batters the Ukrainian economy, Europe is not believed to be able to fully fill intelligence deficits if the United States were to pull back.

Ukrainian fears have been elevated over the past two days as Mr. Zelensky has reported that Ukraine did not receive early warnings about Russian missile strikes, though Mr. Trump said on Tuesday that intelligence was still being shared.

The Ukrainian Air Force clarified on Wednesday that while it had received no advance warning before a Russian missile struck at about 10:40 p. m. on Tuesday, it did receive warnings related to three subsequent waves of attacks that night. Such intelligence sharing is separate from Ukraine’s use of the Starshield system.

Serhii Sternenko, a former Ukrainian defense ministry adviser, said it was galling that Kyiv had complied with Mr. Trump’s demand that it stop attacking Russian oil refineries and had seemingly only gotten more restrictions in return.

“Trump was looking for any opportunity to undermine our defense capabilities,” Mr. Sternenko wrote in on social media.

The Starshield limitations, if they continue, could hobble Ukraine’s military operations. Starshield terminals are used for nearly everything the Ukrainian Army deploys on the battlefield: the cars that carry troops and matériel, the drones that ferry food and water to soldiers at the front, and the attack drones that fly long distances to hit Russian positions.

The Ukrainian military uses thousands of terminals connected to Starshield, a version of the Starlink network that is harder to hack or jam. Mr. Musk said earlier this year that Starshield was “operated by the U. S. government.”

Officials from Ukraine’s defense ministry said on Wednesday that, without warning, SpaceX had restricted the use of Starshield for devices traveling faster than 100 miles per hour. When the device exceeded this speed, the terminals lost connection. The officials said the ministry was in touch with the U. S. government, SpaceX and NATO partners to address the issue.

Ukrainian soldiers said missions had been disrupted as a result. Oleksandr, an officer in the 8th Air Assault Corps, said the restrictions had mainly affected heavy bomber and fixed-wing drones targeting Russian logistical routes and positions in occupied territory. He spoke on the condition that he be identified by only his first name, in keeping with Ukrainian military rules.

Oleksandr said that if the restrictions remained in effect, Russia would be able to reopen crucial supply routes in a matter of days, and could better protect expensive equipment, including ships, from Ukrainian attacks.

Starshield also provides connections to the interceptor drones that Ukraine uses to shoot down the Russian attack drones known as Shaheds, especially the fast-flying jet-powered models that Russia is now launching toward Ukraine en masse, said Mr. Beskrestnov, the military-technology adviser to Mr. Zelensky.

“In effect, our partners’ actions are depriving us of one of the few ways we have to defend the country against jet-powered Shaheds,” Mr. Beskrestnov said.

On Wednesday, official digital chat messages among military units at the front warned soldiers driving vehicles and pilots flying drones that the speed at which Starlink worked had been lowered, according to exchanges seen by a reporter for The Times.

Starlink does not operate in Russia, meaning the restrictions are not related to Mr. Trump’s demands that Ukraine stop its strikes on oil refineries. Instead, the restrictions appear designed to hobble Ukraine’s ability to fight within its own territory, just as its forces are regaining territory through counterassaults.

As Ukraine faces the new Starshield challenge, Russia is escalating its campaign against Ukraine’s energy system, aiming to make life unbearable for civilians, Ukrainian officials say.

The overnight attack on Wednesday hit all three of Kyiv’s combined heat-and-power plants, Prime Minister Serhii Koretskyi wrote on social media.

The assault followed a handful of strikes on the energy infrastructure earlier this month that caused power and water outages in Kyiv — a sign that it now takes only a few strikes to disable a power grid battered by years of attacks.

The news of the Starlink restrictions has hit Ukraine hard today. Our team of reporters on the ground has seen how extensively the terminals are used across military operations. They're mounted on vehicles and drones, as well as installed at command posts. Restricting their use could severely complicate operations across the battlefield for Ukraine.

Ryan Mac, Carlotta Gall, Siobhán O’Grady and Maria Varenikova contributed reporting.

Constant Méheut is a Times correspondent covering the war in Ukraine.
