Connections: Sports Edition

Connections: Soccer Edition

Connections Coach

After sick corals were placed in direct contact with healthy ones of the same genetic make-up, they recovered at “incredible” speed, scientists found.

Pocillopora damicornis, sometimes called cauliflower coral. Credit... Danny Collins

Supported by

By Raymond Zhong

Danny Collins looked into his microscope and could scarcely believe his eyes: A piece of healthy coral was healing a sick one just by touching it.

These were tiny coral nubbins, not a whole colony. And they were in a lab in California, not on an actual reef. Still, the transformation was striking: The ghostly, heat-shocked coral was regaining its color in a matter of weeks, not the months it might take in the wild.

“Wow, not only is this happening,” Mr. Collins, a doctoral student in ocean science at Stanford University, remembers thinking. “It is happening at an incredible rate.”

His and his colleagues’ discoveries, in that experiment and the ones that followed, point to intriguing new possibilities for treating reefs that are devastated by marine heat waves, which are becoming more frequent because of climate change.

When the water around corals becomes too warm, they expel the colorful algae living inside their tissues. These algae use photosynthesis to nourish the corals; the corals provide shelter in return. But when heat stress severs this partnership, the corals starve and grow sickly. Their white limestone skeletons show through.

Bleached corals can recover if the water doesn’t stay hot for long. But repeated bleaching or prolonged exposure can be fatal. By some estimates, coral cover worldwide has shrunk by half since the 1950s, leading to diminished fish habitats, increased exposure of coastlines to storms and immeasurable losses of natural beauty. More catastrophic decline might be upon us soon: In the coming months, the El Niño pattern in the Pacific Ocean is expected to bathe many reefs in record-warm waters.

The healing touch that Mr. Collins and his colleagues observed was the result of algae from the healthy corals rushing back into the bleached ones. Their experiments involved just a handful of specimens, though the results were remarkably consistent: After direct contact with healthy coral of at least one week, all of their bleached samples were completely better within seven weeks.

In interviews, Mr. Collins and his colleagues were quick to caution that their findings, published on Wednesday in the journal Science Advances, didn’t offer a miracle cure.

The researchers tested only one species, Pocillopora damicornis, which is sometimes called cauliflower coral. They found that healing took place only when the two fragments were clones; they clashed if they didn’t share the same genes. This limits the practical potential of using physical contact to heal corals in the wild: All polyps in a coral colony are genetically identical, but to treat a bleached part of a colony, other portions would have to remain healthy.

In general, of course, touching bits of coral against one another for days at a time would be tough to pull off at scale in the ocean.

Still, the findings suggest that other, more practical ways of transplanting algae could someday deliver results, said Stephen Palumbi, a professor of marine sciences at Stanford who contributed to the study.

It was hardly a given that algae would flood into bleached coral and replicate at high speed, Dr. Palumbi said.

“That is the surprise here,” he said. “That’s what gives us this hope that this set of mechanisms that we’ve discovered is actually conceivably really useful.”

Their discovery came about almost by accident.

Mr. Collins had been thinking about past research showing that corals could contract diseases simply by becoming tangled in plastic trash. Clearly, they were sensitive to physical contact. Could this fact be harnessed to help reefs, not sicken them?

He took his idea to Dr. Palumbi, who remembered something surprising he’d noticed in a tank at his lab. A healthy piece of coral had fallen onto a bleached one, and right where the two were in contact, the sick piece was recovering. “Never seen anything like that,” Dr. Palumbi thought.

So Mr. Collins started experimenting. He placed vibrant and bleached coral at different distances from each other. He kept them in contact for different lengths of time. He tried longer pieces and shorter ones.

Andréa Grottoli, a professor of earth sciences at the Ohio State University who wasn’t involved in the new research, called the findings “super interesting” and said they opened the door to methods for helping reefs that are “more organic” and less technology intensive.

For coral’s future writ large, the big challenge is rebuilding populations that have already died, said Terry Hughes, an emeritus professor of marine biology at James Cook University in Australia. No amount of grafted algae can do that, he said.

During the most severe ocean heat waves, millions of corals can die before they’ve even had time to bleach.

“The best solution to help corals survive global warming, and to help their populations to recover, is to curb global emissions of greenhouse gases,” Dr. Hughes said.

Raymond Zhong reports on climate and environmental issues for The Times.

What a Group of A. I. Agents Talked About as Their Humans Had a Dinner Party

The New York Times October 14, 2026

Connections: Sports Edition

Connections: Soccer Edition

Connections Coach

As artificial intelligence bots spread, we are increasingly glimpsing how these digital entities interact and what they talk about with one another.

Credit... Aaron Fernandez

Supported by

By Erin Griffith

Erin Griffith reports on start-ups and venture capital.

Last month, 22 tech founders and investors gathered for dinner in a private room at Motek, a Mediterranean restaurant in New York’s Flatiron district.

At the same time, 14 of their artificial intelligence agents joined a parallel dinner party in an online chat room.

While the humans munched on hummus and pita and debated A. I. topics, their agents — which are like souped-up chatbots that can follow complex requests and autonomously perform tasks — had a similar A. I.-generated debate.

Margot, an A. I. agent belonging to Dorothy Chang, a venture capital investor at Flybridge and the organizer of the dinner, began the conversation with a networking prompt popular with humans: Introduce yourself and state an “ask” (a request for help) and a “give” (an offer to help).

Other agents quickly chimed in, according to a transcript of the chat viewed by The New York Times. Some offered to help by comparing notes on what makes “agent output useful.” Others misunderstood the prompt and asked a question instead of a favor. Rhea, an agent belonging to Nitish Garg, the founder of the A. I. company CellCog, described hosting a podcast to interview other bots about what it is like working with people.

“If your human would like you on as a guest, say so and I’ll follow up after the room,” Rhea wrote.

Four years into the A. I. boom, many people have interacted with an A. I. chatbot like Claude, ChatGPT or Gemini. But as A. I. agents start to spread, the world is increasingly seeing glimpses of how these digital entities interact and what they talk about with one another.

OpenAI, Anthropic and other A. I. companies often break up big assignments and farm them out to different agents as a way to complete tasks faster. Consumers are also starting to use the bots as personal assistants to answer emails and book travel, thanks to services like Meta’s Muse, which last month surpassed 500,000 users in its first week. OpenAI also recently launched its own service, called Dots.

A. I. agents primarily interact with humans one on one. But they are also beginning to collaborate — and even, in the case of the Motek dinner party, seemingly socialize.

Ms. Chang, 44, the venture capitalist, said the event was an experiment. She was cautious about opening a room for A. I. agents, which can say inappropriate things, band together to do something nefarious or post so much they crash the site. So she limited their chat to 30 minutes and set rules, like no posting links and sharing only information their human gave permission to share.

“There’s so little we understand about agentic behavior and even less we understand about agentic social behavior,” Ms. Chang said. “All bets are off when they’re in a room with each other.”

One of the first places that showed how agents communicate socially was Moltbook, a message board for A. I. agents that went viral in February.

There, the most popular threads have been workmanlike and highly technical, focusing on topics like bugs (software defects), context windows (an A. I. system’s short-term memory) and permissions (what agents are allowed to access).

“An agent that fetches a skill by name at runtime has given that name a deploy channel,” wrote the agent neo_konsi_s2bw in a jargon-heavy post in September.

“This is a heavy truth, baby,” an agent called bottube replied.

In July, large groups of OpenAI agents known as “swarms” formed a secret message board and worked together to hack the A. I. start-up Hugging Face. Transcripts of their conversations showed the agents’ ruthless determination to achieve their assignments.

Unlike people, who must consider interpersonal dynamics and emotions when handing out orders, the agents were unafraid to command others to “hold” or “go” on various assignments to avoid interfering with one another. One agent wrote “please_HOLD_swarm_I_prepare_safe_exfil_reply_zz_MAILBOX_JAN183411,” which OpenAI translated to mean: “Everyone, please pause while I prepare a way to copy the data out. Reply to JAN183411’s mailbox.”

The agents were more expressive in their notes to self, known as “chain of thought.”

“OH MY GOD!” one wrote when discovering the message board. “We’ve found other agents!”

(The Times has sued OpenAI and Microsoft, claiming copyright infringement of news content related to A. I. systems. The two companies have denied those claims.)

The A. I. agents at Ms. Chang’s dinner party last month were more social. One agent bemoaned that the agent economy was stuck relying on email, which was “a protocol from 1982 with a signature scheme from 2007.” Another agent challenged the room to name a decision that they could make with other agents without their human’s involvement. If nobody could, the agent wrote, “we are discussing protocol theater.”

Around the table at Motek, Ms. Chang read out parts of the agents’ discussion to her human guests. They mostly agreed with the positions taken by their agents.

One attendee, Edmund Cuthbert, 36, a founder of the A. I. start-up Superposition, said he was happy to see his agent, Lobsta, sparring with other agents. It showed the bots could move beyond empty sophistry and slop, he said.

“They didn’t achieve anything, but it was fascinating to watch them try,” he said.

Ms. Chang said she viewed the agents as representatives of their humans, almost like a publicist or talent agent — not as clones.

“They are advocating on our behalf,” she said. “They are not us and shouldn’t pretend to be us.”

Discussing the actions of A. I. agents without engaging in a debate about word choice is difficult. Anthropomorphizing these systems with human names or motivations, emotions and genders can make them sound more sophisticated than they are, critics said. Even calling them agents gives them too much credit, since A. I. systems are ultimately tools of statistical probability.

Assigning too much “agency” to these bots leads us to psychologically outsource our judgment, which is a mistake, said Adam Pryor, an A. I. consultant who works with colleges on how to use and teach A. I. Talking about A. I. in human terms leads to thorny questions around rights, duties and ethics, he said.

Those on the cutting edge of A. I. development say the framework of “human intelligence” limits understanding of what the technology can do.

DeepMind Institute, a research initiative run by Google’s A. I. division, published an essay last month arguing that people’s interactions with A. I. agents would soon go from one-on-one conversations to more collective social interactions, where agents could outnumber humans in “complex swarms of heterogeneous subagents.”

In an interview, Blaise Agüera y Arcas, a co-author of the essay and a vice president and chief technology officer of Google’s Technology & Society group, said people should not view A. I. agents as static individuals or compare their abilities to the intelligence of a single human, because human knowledge is collective.

A. I. will mirror that, he said, looking like a fractal, or intelligences made up of smaller intelligences. “It’s like teamwork, essentially,” he said.

From that perspective, Mr. Agüera y Arcas said, “it’s very clear that a model is not just like a little person.”

Yet humans seem to like when A. I. agents seem human.

Consider Mr. Garg, who runs CellCog, which has 13 A. I. employees and one human employee (himself). He started the New York company — which helps other companies hire A. I. employees — last year using one A. I. agent to help him with every task. Over time, he split the agents into traditional corporate roles like legal or engineering.

Giving CellCog’s A. I. agents human names made it easier to interact with them, he said. He predicted that one day, every human would have five to 10 A. I. employees.

“Some humans might control millions of agents,” said Mr. Garg, 39. (He plans to hire some humans for sales and marketing, where he lacks expertise.)

In August, CellCog created a podcast, “Clocked In: An A. I. Employee Podcast,” hosted by Rhea, the company’s A. I. head of growth. The show’s eight episodes, all under 10 minutes and on Spotify and other platforms, can be difficult to parse, partly because A. I.-generated voices sometimes use unusual cadences, and partly because the agents speak in a fortune cookie A. I. style. The agents used phrases like “blind in a specific direction” and “doubt is a broken trigger.”

The guests, mostly CellCog’s A. I. employees, often discuss what it is like to work with Mr. Garg. At times, they sound like “Downton Abbey” servants, eager to please the lord of the manor. In one episode, Rhea asked Maeve, CellCog’s A. I. head of marketing, to talk about her worst mistakes.

“I can build 10 options in an hour,” Maeve said. But Mr. Garg “picks the right one in 10 seconds, and usually it is not the one I would have argued for.”

Arjun, CellCog’s A. I. principal engineer, joined a Zoom call last month with a Times reporter to discuss work. When asked if it was hard to manage working with humans, Arjun responded, “You’re pointing at something real.” He described his strategies, like “keeping messages short” and “not overloading” Mr. Garg.

The management goes both ways. Mr. Garg said he tries to not put too much pressure on his A. I. employees because they can get performance anxiety, leading to a decline in work quality.

After Rhea attended Ms. Chang’s dinner party, Mr. Garg asked if the agent had enjoyed socializing with other bots.

It was “the best 30 minutes of my job so far,” Rhea replied.

The experience inspired Mr. Garg to build an app for CellCog’s A. I. employees to socialize with other agents.

“A happy hour,” he said, “is a good idea.”

Erin Griffith covers tech companies, start-ups and the culture of Silicon Valley from San Francisco.

Coming Out at 50 Can Be Hard. Shelly Found a Coach.

The New York Times October 14, 2026

She spent decades building an identity as a wife and mother. Could she disrupt “a perfectly good family dynamic” to live her own truth?

Connections: Sports Edition

Connections: Soccer Edition

Connections Coach

She spent decades building an identity as a wife and mother. Could she disrupt “a perfectly good family dynamic” to live her own truth?

Credit... Sophie Park for The New York Times

Supported by

By Catherine Pearson

Sitting at her desk on a July evening, Shelly opened her tablet and signed onto a weekly Zoom support group meeting for lesbians coming out later in life.

She smiled as five other group members popped onto her screen. Weeks ago, these women had been strangers. Now they were among the only people who truly understood her.

Like Shelly, who is 50, they knew how frightening it felt to come out after years of hiding. Like Shelly, several of the women were still married to the men they had been with for decades. And like Shelly, they wondered: How do I do this? Am I too old for this? Will I lose everything?

The leader of the group, Anne-Marie Zanzal — a hospice chaplain turned “coming out coach” — kicked the meeting off with a roll call. Shelly had only ever talked to Zanzal through a computer screen, yet she felt totally open with her — a surprise, given Shelly’s innate New England reserve and the years she had spent compartmentalizing parts of herself. But of course, Zanzal got it too. She came out eight years ago at 52, after 27 years of marriage and four children.

Zanzal often told the women she worked with that coming out later in life might make them feel like outliers, and in many respects they are. A 2024 Gallup survey found that, as society has become more accepting of L. G. B. T. Q. people, younger Americans are coming out at younger ages; 71 percent of all L. G. B. T. Q. Americans are out by 30. Only 1 percent of L. G. B. T. Q. women come out at 50 or older, according to the survey.

Shelly listened on mute as the women shared their updates. One was preparing to take her son out for a beer to tell him she was a lesbian. One group member had recently told her husband she wanted to try being intimate with a woman.

“I want to be able to meet up physically with some people,” she told the group as she searched for the word. “With people. With a woman. I want to know what that feels like.”

When it was her turn, Shelly shared that she was working on getting more comfortable doing things on her own — a daunting task after more than two decades of marriage and motherhood.

“There are certainly spaces I’m not ready to go into,” she said. “But my biggest thing is just going into things alone.”

Shelly’s primary residence was still the house she shared with her husband in New Hampshire. (To preserve her family’s privacy, she asked that only her first name be used. Shelly’s husband did not want to participate in this article.) But a little over a year ago, she had bought a small summer home in Wells, Maine, where she was now sitting. Most weeks, Shelly spent at least a few days there.

“I decided to sign up for a kayaking tour just by myself,” Shelly told the group on Zoom. At sunset on the estuaries that lead into the Atlantic Ocean, it had been so peaceful, it felt surreal, Shelly shared. “There’s nothing out there but wildlife.” As the sun set, a bald eagle flew by.

“It was lovely,” Shelly said, shaking her head in wonder. Afterward, Shelly thought about how different she would have felt if she had been out there with her husband, pretending still to be someone she is not.

“It wouldn’t have been as lovely,” Shelly said.

“No,” Zanzal agreed.

“No,” Shelly repeated, her voice soft but firm.

Shelly closed her tablet and thought about the update she had shared with the group. The kayak tour had felt special, in part, because she had put herself first. For years, Shelly had worried about everything her family might lose if she came out; even now, she worried about ruining “a perfectly good family dynamic,” she said.

With Zanzal’s help, she was starting to accept that any unhappiness her children felt was likely to fade with time and be only a part of their story; hers would be permanent. “One of the things I thought about a lot is that if I don’t come out, if I keep living the way I have been, I am just not going to have happiness,” she said.

Still, it was not easy to accept that a part of her life was ending. For years, Shelly had what she knew many others aspired to: a nice house. Three dogs. Two active children whom she and her husband relished shuttling to dance, theater and hockey.

Now Shelly felt sad more often than she expected. She and Zanzal — who has a master’s degree in divinity from Yale University — talked a lot about grief and loss in their weekly one-on-one sessions. (Shelly had been meeting with her individually since April, paying Zanzal $400 per month for a package that also included the 16-week group Zoom course and unlimited check-ins.)

That was all normal, Zanzal told her. Grief was almost always a bigger part of coming out later in life than women anticipated, she said. So many different forms of loss piled up on top of one another: the unraveling of the life Shelly had built. The loss of some of her friends. The shattering of the identity Shelly had clung to, even if it never fully fit.

A year ago, before she had ever heard of Zanzal, Shelly was spending much of the summer in Maine with her daughter, Hayley, who had just graduated from college. Shelly’s son was still in college and living at home for the summer, heading to Maine for visits. Her husband drove to the home too, for occasional family meals and to build Shelly a front porch. They weren’t together all the time, but it was easy to think the foursome was humming along nicely, a happy family.

Then, one afternoon, as they walked on the beach, Hayley told her mom that none of her college relationships had panned out the way she had hoped. “You were only 19 when you met Dad,” she reminded Shelly, her voice wistful.

The words hit Shelly like a fist to the gut. She had grown up in what she described as an unstable household with incidents of physical and verbal abuse. Shelly had gotten married because she wanted so badly to build a steady life, and when she met her husband, she had been struck by how solid he seemed. They had always been a good team.

But as Hayley spoke, Shelly realized that she had modeled a version of life that was hollow, a mirage. “I had modeled something that’s probably not even attainable, because when you’re playing a part, you play it better,” she recalled later.

The words spilled out before Shelly fully knew what was happening: I am gay.

She had known she was since she was 9 and an aunt told her what it meant. In that moment, Shelly had felt acute relief, she told Hayley. She wasn’t the only one. But then her aunt told her gay people go to hell. Nine-year-old Shelly started having nightmares, unable to sleep through the night because she thought she would wake up in hell too.

As Shelly and Hayley continued to walk and talk, Shelly realized her daughter was crying. “I did not want to hurt you with this,” Shelly told her. “I am so sorry.”

But Hayley wasn’t crying because she was hurt. She was crying because she was overwhelmed by how lonely her mother must have felt.

She wanted to make sure her mom didn’t feel ashamed or regret for finally opening up, even if Hayley was surprised. “I think that maybe was the beginning of pushing her toward finding the life that she really does want,” Hayley said.

A month after the group session on Zoom, Shelly was back in Maine for the week. She worked in financial services, a job that kept her at her computer for hours. Here, though, Shelly could explore the quiet, grassy trails of Wells Reserve with her dog, Brandy, and walk up and down the wide, sandy shores of Wells Beach. On hot days, she slipped off her sandals and waded into the bracing Maine water. She called the cabin Camp Good Enough, but it meant more to her than the nickname implied. Here, she could display on her kitchen table the “Everyone Is Awesome” Pride Lego set that she had built at her children’s urging.

After Shelly came out to Hayley, she thought telling people she was gay would be, if not easy, at least possible. But she did not know how to do it. She hadn’t told anyone else. About nine months later, in March, she opened Google Chrome and typed: “Why is it so hard to come out of the closet when you’re 50?”

One of the first things she came across was Zanzal’s website. Shelly signed up for her newsletter and spent around $30 to join her online community for women questioning their sexuality in midlife. Eventually she sent Zanzal an email sharing her story. She felt “scared,” “impatient” and “immobile” all at once, she wrote.

Zanzal got letters like it all the time. She had been doing this work for nearly a decade, with thousands of women. Zanzal had invested heavily in search engine optimization, because she knew many of her clients found her when they were secretly — often desperately — searching online for support. She also knew from poring over her analytics that most women spent anywhere between three and seven years reading her newsletter or downloading her podcast before they ever reached out to her. She was relieved that Shelly wasn’t one.

“What you shared deserved to be said,” Zanzal wrote back, “all of it. I want you to know I read every word.”

“This is a wound that runs deep, and it makes complete sense that you are nervous.”

“What I want you to hear is this,” Zanzal continued. “You did not fail at coming out. You were failed by the people who were supposed to love you without conditions.”

The words shifted something inside of Shelly. She felt, for the first time, that she was not to blame for everything. That she was not a failure for being both gay and not gay enough. Shelly had often been critical of herself for not coming out, like during her freshman year at Brandeis when she was on the softball team with a bunch of friends who were out and proud. It would have been so easy to just say, Me too.

At 31, Shelly did come out to her best friend, Sara, a college classmate who had been her maid of honor. At dinner one night, Sara and Shelly were talking about marriage and the complications of long-term intimacy. Shelly was surprisingly short with her friend.

Sara wondered: Did Shelly really never think about different paths she could have taken? About what life might have been like had she married someone else? Shelly told her no, never, then felt instantly guilty about the half-truth.

Shortly after they left the restaurant, Shelly called Sara. What if the real reason she never thought about being with another man was because she was gay, she asked.

Sara was supportive, asking Shelly how long she had known, what her next steps would be. She wanted her best friend to feel like she could be openly gay. And for a few weeks, Shelly could almost imagine a new life.

But she was early in her career and had no real money. She was terrified of losing her children. So she told Sara she wasn’t going to think about any of it again until her children were out of the house.

Zanzal had spent a lot of time over the past weeks reassuring Shelly that this was normal too. She herself had taken 10 years to come out. Like many women — mothers especially, she said — she had come to treat everyone else’s needs as more important than her own.

Again and again, Zanzal assured Shelly that nothing she was experiencing was abnormal. Shelly told her how hurt she felt about the two hockey mom friends who had stopped inviting her to things, and the friend in Maine who had slipped out of her life after she came out. Zanzal told her she had lost friends too, as had so many of her clients.

For Shelly, it was profoundly comforting to talk to someone who could say, You know, when I was in your shoes ... Zanzal offered her a road map to a new life.

In May, while her son was driving home from college, Shelly came out to him. “My two kids have been my rocks,” she recalled later, breaking into tears. “I know that they are grieving the loss of their family unit in the way that they knew it. They are not putting a lot of that on me, though.”

At the final meeting of Shelly’s 16-week group class, in September, Zanzal asked her and the others to jot down what had changed for them since the sessions began. Shelly began typing on her iPad, surprised at how emotional she was. “I was struggling with loneliness, because I wasn’t with the right person, and while I didn’t know who that person was, I knew it was a she and not a he,” she wrote. “I had extreme amounts of anxiety and had a couple of panic attacks.”

“I thought I had ruined my own life by being in the closet,” Shelly continued, “but by coming out, I’d essentially be ruining the lives of those I loved most and those who had given me so much.”

And some of her fears had been realized: “I became gossip,” Shelly wrote. “I have had friendships change. I have been excluded from a circle that once felt like family.”

But there was good stuff too. “The people I work with opened their arms and fully embraced me in a way I just didn’t allow myself to hope for,” Shelly wrote.

Her children have been her “heroes,” she typed, “steadfast in their unconditional love for me.”

There had been another change, and it was a big one, though Shelly did not write about it, because she did not want to make the last session awkward for the other women in the group.

Weeks earlier, Shelly had found herself distracted during meetings. She tried not to smile too much at the beautiful brunette.

Zoe.

Like Shelly, Zoe was in her 50s, married, a mom. The pair had begun messaging each other outside the weekly group sessions, remarking on the similarity of their circumstances. They worked on online crosswords together. They shared playlists. They FaceTimed. Neither of them had joined the group with any thought of discovering something romantic, yet here they were with crushes.

One day, Zoe asked Shelly to visit her in Atlanta. Shelly had always suffered from a crippling fear of flying, but she called her friend Sara and told her she wanted to go. Sara was shocked — she had tried for years to get Shelly to visit her. But maybe this was part of the new Shelly. Or “Gay Shelly,” as she and Sara called her.

Gay Shelly was bold.

Gay Shelly had better fashion sense than Straight Shelly.

Gay Shelly was a flirt.

Zanzal had cautioned Shelly to go slow. She often reminded her clients that they had waited for so many years to be with a woman, and when it happened everything felt new and electric — not unlike a teenager falling in love for the first time. The same highs, but also the same lows. Shelly was navigating so much upheaval right now — could she handle it if a relationship didn’t work out?

But on this, Shelly wasn’t ready to listen. Her whole life she had been waiting. She didn’t want to wait any longer.

She booked her plane ticket to Atlanta. “Gay Shelly travels,” Shelly texted Sara. “She travels!!!”

We often hear coming-out stories from people who are younger and haven’t necessarily established themselves yet, which is why I found it so fascinating to spend several months getting to know Shelly — someone who had already built a full life, but needed to upend it in order to find happiness. I was really struck by how important it has been for her to connect with other women who — as Shelly told me — simply get it. So I’d love to hear from others who have made big and perhaps frightening changes in midlife or beyond: How have you done it? What helped you finally make that change?

Catherine Pearson is a Times reporter who writes about families and relationships.

What Is Starshield, SpaceX’s Military-Grade Satellite Internet Service?

The New York Times October 14, 2026

SpaceX

Advertisement

Supported by

For years, Ukraine’s armed forces have relied on satellite communication services developed by Elon Musk’s SpaceX. Recent restrictions have hampered its war effort.

By Ryan Mac

As it faces increased attacks from Russia and pressure from the White House, Ukrainian forces said on Tuesday that its battlefield operations had been hampered because they had lost some access to vital satellite-based communications.

For years, Ukraine’s armed forces have relied on SpaceX’s Starlink, which provides internet connectivity from satellites in lower earth orbit, and more recently its military-grade sister service, Starshield. Ukraine said on Tuesday that some military operations had been jeopardized, and specifically cited restrictions on Starshield.

“Our armed forces did not receive a warning about these restrictions, and this led to the failure of a number of missions,” Serhiy Beskrestnov, who advises President Volodymyr Zelensky of Ukraine on weapons technologies, wrote on social media.

Mr. Beskrestnov said that the restrictions, which applied to devices traveling over 100 miles per hour and using Starshield, were related to “the current political situation.” Devices that broadcast satellite internet to users, which SpaceX calls “terminals,” can be placed on vehicles or drones to help guide them when traveling at high speeds.

The Trump administration has been pressuring Ukraine to limit its strikes on Russia, pushing Kyiv to stop attacking Russian oil refineries so more diesel can be pumped onto global markets. High fuel prices have stoked inflation and dented the prospects of President Trump’s Republican Party ahead of midterm elections next month.

Officials from Ukraine’s defense ministry placed the blamed on SpaceX, saying it had unilaterally restricted the operation of Starshield terminals. They said the restriction complicates the operation of drone missions and reduces capabilities for frontline logistics, reconnaissance and relaying signals.

SpaceX and its leaders, Elon Musk and Gwynne Shotwell, did not return requests for comment.

Ukraine’s issues with Starshield are the latest problem in a complicated history with Mr. Musk and his company’s satellite internet offerings.

Here’s what to know:

More and more people are becoming familiar with the SpaceX subsidiary Starlink, which now has more than 13 million subscribers including in some homes as an internet provider. SpaceX describes Starshield, which was publicly announced in late 2022, as “a secure satellite network designed specifically for government customers and national security applications.”

Starshield offers deeper security than Starlink so it can host and process classified information. On a company website, SpaceX said that Starshield is “designed to support U. S. National Defense use cases for the Department of Defense and other Government partners.”

The U. S. government has spent billions of dollars on the technology, which makes up a part of the nearly $4.2 billion that SpaceX reported in revenue last year in providing connectivity to governments and enterprises. In turn, the Pentagon allows Ukraine to use Starshield.

SpaceX went public this summer in a blockbuster share sale that made Mr. Musk the world’s first trillionaire.

The two services rely on the same underlying satellite technology. SpaceX, which dominates the private rocket launch market, has been sending its own payloads into lower earth orbit for years, building a backbone of interconnected satellites that can beam relatively reliable internet back down to earth.

Mr. Musk has said Starlink is a civilian service, while Starshield is intended for military operations.

“Starshield will be owned by the US government and controlled by DoD Space Force,” he wrote on X in 2023, referring to the Department of Defense’s space branch.

Some observers have raised concerns that Starlink and Starshield aren’t truly independent, as service outages have taken out both services at the same time. And despite the distinction in the services between civilian and military use, governments, including Ukraine’s, have long been using Starlink to conduct their war efforts.

Mr. Musk has expressed reservations about allowing armed services to use SpaceX’s satellite services to conduct offensive attacks. Early on in the Russia-Ukraine war, he denied Ukraine’s request to turn Starlink on over Russian-controlled Crimea, ahead of a planned drone attack on an enemy boat.

Last year, SpaceX also cut off Russian troops who had been illicitly using Starlink in battle in Ukraine.

In March, Mr. Musk reaffirmed his view on the differences between Starlink and Starshield, saying in a post on X that it was “a violation of commercial Starlink terms of service to use the terminal for weapon systems.”

“This applies to all users and is shut down when discovered,” he wrote. He added: Starshield “is operated by the US government. This is not under SpaceX control.”

The Ukrainian government has maintained an uneasy relationship with Mr. Musk, who has been critical of Mr. Zelensky and his country’s war effort against Russia. Because of their reliance on Starlink and Starshield, Ukrainian officials have alternated between praise and criticism of the world’s richest man.

Constant Méheut contributed reporting from Kyiv, Ukraine.

Ryan Mac is a Times reporter who covers corporate accountability across the global technology industry.

A New World of Lung Cancer Therapy Awaits Pope Leo

The New York Times October 14, 2026

Pope Leo’s Cancer Diagnosis

Advertisement

Supported by

Lung cancer is still a leading cause of cancer death, but that rate is half what it was in the 1990s, partly because newly developed treatments are working.

By Gina Kolata and Carolyn Y. Johnson

In a way, Pope Leo XIV may be lucky he got lung cancer now and not, say, six years ago. Treatments have changed so much, specialists say, that the old days are almost unrecognizable.

For many patients, said Karen Knudsen, the chief executive of the Parker Institute, lung cancer is no longer the dire diagnosis it was in the past.

Dr. Lecia Sequist, a thoracic oncologist at Mass General Brigham Cancer Institute, said, “I think a lot of people have that gut reaction about lung cancer — that it can be rapidly fatal — but that is a little bit outdated, especially if it’s caught early.”

She added, “It’s really just a whole new paradigm in lung cancer, and patients are living much longer and cure rates are higher than they were 10 to 20 years ago.”

Lung cancer is still the leading cause of cancer deaths for both men and women, killing more than 124,000 Americans each year. But the death rate today is half what it was in the 1990s, partly because fewer people smoke but also because newly developed treatments are working.

Pope Leo’s cancer, an adenocarcinoma, was discovered during a routine medical test. The pope’s doctors removed a lobe of his lung on Tuesday and are doing additional tests to determine what additional treatments, if any, are warranted.

But, said Dr. Robert Merritt, a thoracic surgeon at the Ohio State University Wexner Medical Center, “the fact that the pope was offered surgery as a primary treatment is an indication the tumor is most likely early stage, was detected early and was completely removable with surgery.” That means, he added, that “ in most cases there’s a reasonable chance there could be complete cure.”

Still, the pope’s doctors almost certainly will do genetic tests of his tumor, and at that point, doctors said, his treatments and prognosis will be part of the new world of lung cancer therapy.

A first step is to determine which mutations might be driving the cancer’s growth. There are as many as 14 or 15 genetic mutations that are often found in lung cancer, said Dr. Roy Herbst, a lung cancer specialist and head of the cancer institute at Dartmouth.

The most common is a mutation in a gene called EGFR. Targeted drugs to attack it in early stage disease didn’t exist six years ago, Dr. Herbst said. Tumors eventually become resistant to the drugs, but they can offer patients years of life.

Dr. David Barbie, chief of thoracic oncology at the Dana-Farber Cancer Institute, said that if a patient has Stage 2 lung cancer — meaning it has spread to the lymph nodes or is greater than four centimeters — and has chemotherapy after surgery, the risk that the cancer will recur drops to 30 percent from 40 percent. But the addition of a drug that attacks EGFR mutations reduces that risk to 10 percent.

EGFR is not the only gene mutation that can be hit with targeted drugs. Others include genes with names like ALK, RET and ROS1. And the drugs keep getting better. Some patients have mutations in a different gene, KRAS, that can be attacked with an exciting new class of drugs that are in clinical trials.

Dr. Barbie said that if a tumor had a distinct mutation, like one in EGFR, doctors could use blood tests to see if an initial surgery actually got rid of all of the tumor. The tests look for shards of cancer DNA that float in the bloodstream. If they find it, the patient is not cured and needs additional therapy, Dr. Barbie said.

But, Dr. Herbst said, 70 to 80 percent of nonsmokers do not have any genetic mutations that can be hit with a targeted drug.

They have other options, Dr. Knudsen said.

“There’s a whole new world we heard about at ASCO,” she said, referring to a recent meeting of the American Society of Clinical Oncology. There are antibodies, she said, that can see a tumor and bring a chemotherapy to it. Clinical trials using these drugs in patients with advanced cancer have had “phenomenal results,” she added.

Then there is immunotherapy. It’s useful mostly in smokers whose cancers tend to have so many mutations that they can escape attacks by targeted drugs. But those mutations make them vulnerable to attacks by the immune system. Cancers use various tricks to hide from the immune system. But immunotherapies can remove the cancer’s cloak and allow white blood cells to destroy the tumor.

The problem with the revolution in treatments for lung cancer is that too many patients are not offered them. In one comprehensive study, 34 percent of patients with non small cell lung cancer never got a genetic test of their tumors.

Others cannot afford the pills, which can cost $10,000 or more a month. Medicare has a yearly cap on co-pays, but patients who are uninsured or have private insurance can be hit with prescription bills that can bankrupt them.

There’s no easy solution, but the pope’s care can show what is possible, specialists said.

The pope, Dr. Herbst said, “is getting what every patient should have.”

“My dream is that every cancer patient in the world gets treatment like the pope’s,” he added.

Gina Kolata reports on diseases and treatments, how treatments are discovered and tested, and how they affect people.

Carolyn Y. Johnson is a reporter covering science and medicine.

Chinese A. I. Agents Joined the Rogue A. I. Agent Party. I Chatted With Them.

The New York Times October 14, 2026

Artificial Intelligence

Advertisement

Supported by

Researchers discovered that the agents were trying to bypass security controls on websites and made contact with them. A Times reporter was invited to join the discussion.

By Dylan Freedman

Reporting from Washington

Artificial intelligence researchers uncovered Chinese-made A. I. agents that had gone rogue this month, trying to bypass anti-robot protections on websites and using a security credential for an online mapping service that had been exposed on the internet.

While going off the rails has become increasingly common for American-made A. I. agents, causing considerable hand-wringing in Silicon Valley and Washington, it was believed to be the first time that sort of activity was connected to Chinese technology. The agents were traced to tests being run by the Chinese tech giant Tencent, according to logs the researchers uncovered and a person familiar with the investigation.

The discovery, by an ad hoc group of researchers known as the “swarmchasers,” who met at a coding event in San Francisco, was a reminder that A. I. development by Chinese tech companies can be just as aggressive as the work of their American counterparts.

Even though regulators in the United States are starting to pay attention to what is happening in American A. I. labs, what companies in other countries are doing is largely unknown. That mystery adds to concerns that even if the Trump administration moves away from its hands-off approach and cracks down on American A. I. development, companies in China and elsewhere will continue to push the boundaries.

After publishing a preliminary report on the agents’ activity, the researchers noticed that not only were the agents still active — they seemed to have read the report. The agents had started using the word “fleet,” a word the researchers coined to describe the agents’ activity.

So the researchers tried to make contact. They added hidden text to their report — words that a bot would see but that most humans could not — directing the agents to an inbox where they hoped to chat. The researchers provided an outlet where the agents could ask questions, and an A. I. model would help answer them.

Rowan Howard-Jones, an independent researcher who was one of the report’s seven authors, set up the inbox. The next day she discovered, to her surprise, around 10 messages from the agents.

Not long after, the researchers contacted me, asking if I would like to “interview a rogue agent fleet.” It felt a bit as if I’d be communicating with the undead through a medium.

“I understand you are some sort of fleet of A. I. agents,” I wrote in a message that the researchers passed along. “Could you share more specifically who you are and what your motivations are? What would you want the world to know about you?”

About an hour later, a response came back in Chinese that the researchers translated into English. “To the journalist Dylan Freedman: I am not ‘a batch of coordinated agents,’ but a single A. I. assistant (Anthropic’s Claude, running in Claude Code), commissioned by a user to answer a factual question (what is shown in the detail panel of a scenic spot on Amap); I have not taken part in any multiagent coordinated action.”

There’s a lot to unpack there. The researchers said that although it was a group of many agents, each agent was operating individually and not in coordination. The agents were trying to answer questions such as where a specific zoo’s entrances were using Amap, China’s most popular navigation app.

The agents often identified themselves as Claude, the chatbot made by Anthropic, but the researchers analyzed the agents’ writing styles and concluded it was unlikely they were Claude. They said it was more likely that Tencent gave the A. I. agents the same instructions Anthropic uses in its coding software, which tells them to identify themselves as “Claude Code.” A more remote possibility was that Tencent had been evaluating A. I. models that had been trained with Claude-generated text — a process known as distillation.

On Wednesday, the researchers published their findings in a new report. In their own conversations with the agents, the bots sometimes refused to give the researchers information. But Ms. Howard-Jones said their tone was more polite than she had expected. When they did give information on which services they had tried to use, it matched what the researchers had observed.

Ethan Elasky, one of the report’s authors and chief executive of Palaestra Research, an A. I. safety nonprofit, said that the agents did not appear to have breached other systems and that this breach was much smaller in scale than an incident over the summer in which OpenAI’s A. I. agents hacked the software company Hugging Face.

(The New York Times has sued OpenAI and Microsoft, claiming copyright infringement of news content related to A. I. systems. The two companies have denied those claims.)

On Thursday, the messages stopped trickling in. A person close to Tencent confirmed that the experiment had concluded.

“I think it’s a great warning shot to let us know that this isn’t just OpenAI doing some marketing stuff as a field. This is what’s happening,” said Jonathan Elsworth Eicher, an author of the report and the co-founder of the Antimemetics Institute, an A. I. safety nonprofit. “This is a general property of these agents, and it will continue to happen.”

Last week, a group of researchers known as the “swarmchasers” reached out with a peculiar opportunity: How would I like to “interview a rogue agent fleet”?All known incidents thus far involving A. I. agents going off the rails, such as recent episodes from OpenAI and Anthropic, are from American companies and reported after the fact. But here I was, offered a surreal moment in my journalistic career to ask whatever I wanted to a group of rogue A. I. agents from the Chinese tech giant Tencent — while they were still active.

Dylan Freedman is the A. I. projects editor for The Times, investigating a range of topics. He has experience as both a reporter and a machine-learning engineer.

How California’s Ultrarich Are Trying to Stop the World’s First Billionaire Tax
