Connections: Sports Edition

Connections: Soccer Edition

Connections Coach

Billionaires feel like they “have become piñatas for politicians.” Instead of taking a hit, they’re swinging back.

Credit... Illustration by Doug Chayka

Supported by

By Michael Steinberger

Michael Steinberger spoke to scholars, politicians, labor leaders and several billionaires and millionaires for this article.

Control of Congress is the biggest issue at stake in November’s elections. A close second might be the fight over a ballot initiative in California, Proposition 40, that seeks to impose a wealth tax on the state’s roughly 250 billionaires. If approved, it will be the world’s first billionaire tax.

At a town hall in August, one of its chief proponents, Representative Ro Khanna, whose district encompasses much of Silicon Valley, asked how many in the audience were in favor of the measure. Dozens of hands quickly shot up — an overwhelming majority of those in the room. That Proposition 40 enjoyed such strong support was telling. Even here, in one of the most affluent parts of the country, anger over economic inequality was at a boil.

Khanna played to the populist fury that evening, making light of the fact that none of the billionaires who vociferously oppose the tax were present. “They expect a one-on-one meeting. They expect you to go drive to them,” he said mockingly. “How dare they actually be a commoner and sit in a seat in a town hall and wait to be called on!”

Khanna, a 50-year-old Democrat running for a sixth term, had easily fended off a primary challenge in June from a tech entrepreneur who was against Proposition 40. But when I spoke with him before the town hall, Khanna said his endorsement of the tax had been politically costly in other ways. “I’ve lost at least 20 percent of my donor base,” he said. That included several tech billionaires who had previously contributed to his campaigns. It puzzled Khanna that they couldn’t recognize what seemed self-evident to him: that the tax was actually in their best interest.

Khanna recalled that when he was growing up, people like Steve Jobs and Lee Iacocca were seen as heroes. But now, tech barons and other business leaders are widely loathed. “Eric Schmidt’s getting booed at commencements,” he said of the former chief executive of Google.

In his view, the wealth tax — a one-time 5 percent fee on total worldwide assets — is modest and a small price to pay if it helps blunt some of the rage directed at billionaires. Khanna’s message to them was simple: Read the room. “Mamdani or the next generation want your heads,” he said. “They are coming with pitchforks.”

Michael Moritz, a Silicon Valley titan, is acutely aware of how unpopular he and other billionaires are. “Every time I see a story with ‘billionaire’ in the headline, I know it is meant as a swipe,” he told me when I visited him at his San Francisco home the following day. Billionaires, he said, “have become piñatas for politicians.”

A former venture capitalist, Moritz backed many notable start-ups, including Google, and has an estimated net worth of around $8 billion. As we talked in his living room, which offered a panoramic view of San Francisco Bay and the Marin headlands, he acknowledged that economic inequality was a major problem and said America’s wealth gap “needs to be narrowed in some manner.” To that end, he said he favored eliminating the loopholes that enable the ultrarich to substantially reduce their tax burdens. But he was adamant that Proposition 40 amounted to “asset seizure” and was rooted in resentment and spite. “California, sadly, has become a state that doesn’t value success anymore,” he said.

Moritz was certain that the billionaire tax, if enacted, would leave California worse off. “It’s only going to wind up, in my opinion, hurting the people that they’re trying to help,” he said, predicting that many of the state’s highest earners would flee, eroding California’s tax base. He personally would not join any exodus, but that was only because of his age. “I’m going to be 73 next month,” he said. “Our kids are here. I think if I was 30 years younger and I was running a successful company, I’d probably move.”

Sergey Brin, the Google co-founder, may have already left. Late last year, he bought a mansion on the Nevada side of Lake Tahoe, apparently to escape the possible tax. But he is also now leading the fight in California against Proposition 40 and has committed more than $100 million to that effort. In April, he explained to The Times why he was so determined to defeat the ballot measure. “I fled socialism with my family in 1979 and know the devastating, oppressive society it created in the Soviet Union,” said Brin, who is 53. “I don’t want California to end up in the same place.”

While Proposition 40 has galvanized Brin and other billionaires, it has divided California Democrats. Khanna’s ardent support for the tax has put him at odds with Gov. Gavin Newsom, who tried to prevent it from reaching the ballot. Newsom’s likely successor as governor, Xavier Becerra, who served as health secretary in the Biden administration, is also opposed to the tax.

Progressives and Democratic-aligned organizations are split, too, even though an “eat the rich” sentiment runs strong on the left. One of the main sticking points is how the revenue would be spent. Proposition 40 is sponsored by California’s largest health care workers’ union, and most of the $100 billion it is projected to raise will go to health care programs imperiled by federal cuts.

A number of advocacy groups think the money should be disbursed more widely and have refused to back the measure. These disagreements have been exacerbated, it seems, by the health care union’s president, Dave Regan, a polarizing figure known for his aggressive tactics as well as his frequent involvement with ballot initiatives (nearly 50 since 2009, according to Politico).

If Regan is the political muscle behind Proposition 40, the brains behind it are a pair of French economists, Emmanuel Saez and Gabriel Zucman. Both are on the faculty at the University of California, Berkeley, and have devoted their careers to studying inequality and tax avoidance by the rich. They served as informal advisers to Senator Elizabeth Warren when she ran for president in 2020, and policymakers here and abroad regularly consult with them.

Saez and Zucman believe that we are living in a second Gilded Age; that the extreme concentration of wealth at the very top is as much a political calamity as it is an economic injustice; and that reining in the fortunes and influence of billionaires and multimillionaires is imperative. To them, Proposition 40 is a referendum on the allocation of wealth in the United States but also the distribution of power. As Saez puts it, “Are we still a democracy, or are we going to be an oligarchy?”

He and other supporters of Proposition 40 think it could be a bellwether, heralding a shift away from the untrammeled capitalism that has enabled a small group of individuals to amass outlandishly large fortunes. They cite as precedent an earlier upheaval that also began in California. In 1978, the state’s voters approved Proposition 13, which placed a cap on property taxes. That so-called tax-revolt initiative presaged Ronald Reagan’s election as president in 1980 and was arguably the first shot in the Reagan Revolution. Now, nearly a half-century later, Californians will be voting on a measure that its architects hope will lead to another reordering of American economic and political life — this time in favor of the have-nots and the left behind.

See how America's billionaires can accumulate so much wealth without drawing a significant salary.

The One Big Beautiful Bill Act, championed and signed by President Trump last year, included tax cuts that were partly offset by sharp reductions in social programs. California stands to lose around $100 billion in federal Medicaid funding over the next five years, threatening health care coverage for millions of low-income residents, as well as the potential closure of hospitals and other facilities. To guard against these outcomes and help protect the livelihoods of caregivers, Regan’s union, the Services Employees International Union-United Healthcare Workers West, which has 120,000 members, sponsored Proposition 40. But the idea of a California wealth tax originated with a different trade union during the pandemic.

With the U. S. economy in lockdown, there was grave concern about the fiscal impact at the state level. Anticipating a budget crunch and fearing job losses and pay cuts, the California Federation of Teachers reached out to Saez and Zucman in the spring of 2020 to see if they could help devise a tax that might make up for any shortfall. The two economists had been working with the Warren campaign. They had also recently published a book, “The Triumph of Injustice: How the Rich Dodge Taxes and How to Make Them Pay,” which made the case for a global wealth tax.

Saez, who is 53, earned his doctorate at M. I. T. and joined the Berkeley faculty in 2002. His interest in wealth and inequality only grew after he relocated to Northern California. “You had homeless people in the streets here in Berkeley, close to this booming Silicon Valley, in a way that was shocking,” he recalls. Zucman, who is 39, was hired by Berkeley in 2015. While he remains on the faculty there, he is now at the Paris School of Economics during the academic year. Saez and Zucman have each won the John Bates Clark Medal, the second most prestigious honor in economics and often a precursor to the most prestigious: the Nobel Prize.

The pair had provided input to Warren on the federal wealth-tax plan that became a centerpiece of her White House bid. Senator Bernie Sanders, who was also running for president in 2020, likewise sought their counsel when he was developing a wealth-tax proposal of his own. Warren and Sanders prompted a national conversation around wealth taxes and helped make Zucman and Saez stars in the policy world. Critics contend that they overstate the extent of our current inequality and that their research suffers from methodological flaws and ideological bias. Saez and Zucman vigorously dispute those charges, and their principal claim — that the fortunes of the superrich have exploded and that most of this wealth goes untaxed — seems incontestable.

Any serious debate about the tax question was put to rest in 2021, when the I. R. S. documents of some of America’s best-known billionaires were obtained and published by ProPublica. What they revealed was startling. Amazon’s founder, Jeff Bezos, to take one example, saw his net worth increase by almost $100 billion from 2014 to 2018. During that same period, he declared total income of $4.22 billion, on which he paid $973 million in federal taxes. What ProPublica called his “true” tax rate — the rate that Bezos paid on all the wealth he accrued during this period — was just 0.98 percent.

Bezos was able to pay so little because most of his wealth consisted of Amazon stock, and under the U. S. tax code, his gains are taxed only if he sells shares. But because billionaires can fund their lifestyles by borrowing against their assets, they have little need to ever unload the stocks they own (and it’s worth noting that the money they borrow isn’t taxed, either). Moreover, if Bezos gifts his Amazon holdings to his heirs, his gains will never be taxed, and unless his beneficiaries sell, gains that they subsequently enjoy won’t be taxed, either.

“The tax system has become very efficient at leaving out the types of wealth that the wealthy have,” says Ray Madoff, a Boston College law professor and the author of a recently published book titled “The Second Estate: How the Tax Code Made an American Aristocracy.” “We’re letting massive amounts of wealth go untaxed.”

What’s true at the federal level is also true at the state level. According to Saez and Zucman, neither Sergey Brin nor Larry Page, the Google co-founders, paid any California income tax on their Google wealth in 2019, 2020 and 2023, even though the combined value of their stakes in the company increased by more than $60 billion. Brin and Page weren’t cheating the system; they were simply benefiting from it. During those three years, neither of them took a salary from Google, nor did they sell any Google shares or receive any dividend payments. Their net worth soared because of their Google stock, but because they didn’t draw any income from the company, they paid no income tax on the added wealth that they derived from Google.

After Saez and Zucman agreed to help the California Federation of Teachers in 2020, they recruited two law professors who specialize in tax policy, David Gamage and Darien Shanske, to assist them in drafting a wealth tax. (Another law professor, Brian Galle, who teaches at Berkeley, later joined the effort.) The group came up with a plan for a one-time 0.4 percent tax on the net worth of all Californians with more than $30 million in assets excluding real estate holdings. They left those out in order to comply with restrictions imposed by Proposition 13 that are still in place; for the same reason, real estate is also excluded from the billionaire tax. Rob Bonta, now California’s attorney general but at the time a state assemblyman, sponsored a bill to establish the tax. But it never came to a vote, in part because California weathered the pandemic better than expected.

Trump’s One Big Beautiful Bill brought renewed interest to the tax that Zucman, Saez and their colleagues had designed, this time from the health care workers’ union. As Saez recalls, “Dave Regan came in and said: We have an emergency. We need revenue.”

The two economists and the trio of law professors revived the 2020 wealth tax, but with a twist: They believed that California could raise the $100 billion needed to make up for federal cuts with a one-time wealth tax applied to a much smaller segment of the state’s population: its 250 or so billionaires. The tax could be limited to them because their wealth had exploded in recent years. Brin was worth $49 billion in 2020, according to Forbes. Since then, Google’s stock has roughly quintupled, and he is now worth around $237 billion. Page has done even better: Since 2020, his net worth has jumped to more than $280 billion, from $50 billion.

A few months ago, Bezos was interviewed on CNBC and grew testy when asked about his tax bill. “You could double the taxes I pay,” he said, “and it’s not going to help that teacher in Queens, I promise you.” Zucman and Saez say that is flatly untrue. In an age of centibillionaires, taxing even just a fraction of their aggregate wealth would make a material difference to society. “The revenue implications would be large,” Zucman says.

He cites California as an example. The billionaires who reside there now have a combined net worth of $2.4 trillion, equivalent to about half the state’s G. D. P. (and California by itself is the world’s fifth-biggest economy). If Proposition 40 passes and the tax yields the projected $100 billion in revenue, the total net worth of those billionaires will decline to just under $2.3 trillion. What amounts to a rounding error for them could preserve health care coverage for millions.

Saez admits that levying the tax only on billionaires was a strategic choice. As he dryly observes, “They are no longer very popular.” Regan told me that when his union conducted focus groups before rolling out Proposition 40, the idea of targeting billionaires generated marked enthusiasm. “That made a difference to voters,” he says. To get the measure on the ballot, the union had to obtain 874,641 signatures. It ended up with 1.6 million.

Regan insists that his goal is not to take down billionaires. “The mission was, How do we prevent the collapse of the health care system?” he says. “We’re not trying to win a debate about the rightness or wrongness of billionaire wealth.” Tapping that wealth, though, was an obvious solution to an urgent problem. “It’s the Willie Sutton thing,” Regan says. “Why do I rob banks? Because that’s where the money is.”

At a holiday party last year hosted by Chris Larsen, a crypto billionaire, Brin took Newsom aside to complain about the tax that Regan’s union was seeking to put on the ballot. Brin and the governor were old friends who attended each other’s weddings, but according to an account in The Times, Brin made his indignation clear.

Around the same time, Brin expressed his displeasure in another way: He purchased a $42 million mansion in Incline Village, Nev. Brin also began organizing an effort to defeat Proposition 40. He enlisted the support of other billionaires, among them Michael Moritz, Eric Schmidt and John Doerr, and helped start a nonprofit called Building a Better California, which has now turned a fire hose of money against Proposition 40.

Building a Better California is pursuing a multipronged strategy. It succeeded in putting two other measures before the voters, Propositions 41 and 42. The goal, it appears, was to make a crowded ballot even more confusing: There are now 14 different initiatives under consideration this November. But Propositions 41 and 42 also contain provisions that would effectively prohibit California from introducing a wealth tax, and if either one gets more votes than Proposition 40, it will prevail even if 40 is approved. (Newsom has endorsed Proposition 42.) Building a Better California is spending tens of millions of dollars on ads in support of Propositions 41 and 42. Along with other groups, it is also waging a frontal assault on the wealth tax, flooding the airwaves and social media feeds with ads denouncing it.

Not every California billionaire opposes Proposition 40. In January, Nvidia’s chief executive, Jensen Huang, whose net worth has soared to around $200 billion on the back of the A. I. revolution, told Bloomberg that he was “perfectly fine” with the tax.

But Huang is an outlier, especially in Silicon Valley, where the overwhelming reaction has been one of anger and alarm. A source of particular consternation is that the tax will apply to “paper” billionaires — to the heads of start-ups that haven’t yet gone public but whose personal wealth, by virtue of their ownership stakes, is valued in excess of $1 billion. The argument against taxing them as “real” billionaires is that they may not have cash on hand to pay and that the valuations are just well-educated guesstimates.

In August, Ro Khanna addressed this issue on X, saying that those short on cash could borrow money from the state to pay the tax, pledging shares in their companies as collateral. This drew a furious response from the billionaire Mark Cuban, who called the idea “insane.” Cuban, a resident of Texas, claimed that it would destroy the growth prospects of new companies. If Proposition 40 passed, he wrote on X, “only idiot startup founders stay in Cali.”

Garry Tan, the chief executive of Y Combinator, which funds and advises tech start-ups, was equally caustic when I spoke with him recently. “These are speculative start-ups,” he told me. “Markets can shift very quickly. You can bankrupt a founder.” Tan, although not a billionaire himself, is close to many of Silicon Valley’s leading figures. He suggested that Proposition 40 was “targeting tech in a very fundamental way” and would crush California’s entrepreneurial spirit if it became law. “I think we’re on the verge of strangling the golden goose,” Tan said.

The academics who designed the wealth tax contend that the objections of Tan and Cuban amount to a red herring. They point out that “paper” billionaires, like “true” billionaires, are able to borrow against their assets to cover living expenses. If they are treated like billionaires by banks and other financial institutions, the argument goes, they should be by the state too. Proposition 40 also gives individuals who are subject to the tax the option of paying it over a five-year period. In addition, Zucman notes, start-up founders who have more than 95 percent of their net worth tied up in a business can defer payment until the company has a “liquidity event” (that is, goes public or issues dividends).

Proposition 40 supporters accuse the tech community of fear-mongering. They insist that a one-time tax imposed on just 250 people is highly unlikely to destroy the advantages that Silicon Valley has built up over decades. During his town hall, Khanna cited data showing that 85 percent of recent U. S. venture-capital investment went to businesses in California. He said this was because “we have all the talent that’s leading to the A. I. revolution” and predicted that a “vast majority” of California’s billionaires would stay even if Proposition 40 was approved.

But part of the reason people in tech circles are issuing such apocalyptic warnings is that they don’t believe that the wealth tax would be just a one-time event. “Have you ever heard of a one-time tax?” Moritz asked me. The skepticism is not unwarranted: This year, Khanna sponsored a bill with Sanders to establish an annual federal wealth tax, and Zucman and Saez have made clear that they see Proposition 40 as only a start. A widely shared view in Silicon Valley is that the billionaire tax, if it goes into effect, will be made permanent and that the dynamism that birthed Apple, Google and other era-defining companies will migrate to places like Miami and Austin, Texas.

That’s exactly what Newsom has said he fears. A few weeks after his encounter with Brin at the holiday party, the governor announced that he, too, was against Proposition 40. “This will be defeated,” he told The Times in January. “There’s no question in my mind.” He added, “I’ll do what I have to do to protect the state.” According to Regan, Newsom personally pleaded with him in the spring to abandon the proposed tax. But Regan refused, and Newsom failed to stop it from qualifying for the ballot.

For the two-term governor, Proposition 40 has become a tripwire. Newsom has long enjoyed cordial relations with Brin and other tech executives and is eager not to alienate potential donors ahead of a possible presidential run. At the same time, though, the country is seething with economic discontent, and moguls like Brin have become objects of scorn. Being perceived as soft on billionaires is likely to be a political liability for any Democrat seeking the White House.

In June, the day after Proposition 40 was formally approved for the ballot, Newsom issued a statement reiterating his opposition to the measure but also announcing that he was in favor of a national wealth tax. Triangulation, however, didn’t buy him any more sway in the roiling debate over Proposition 40: In August, the executive board of the California Democratic Party defied Newsom and voted to endorse the billionaire tax. (Newsom’s office didn’t respond to requests for an interview.)

Prominent California Democrats who are also dubious of Proposition 40 include Xavier Becerra and Dan Lurie, San Francisco’s mayor. The state’s U. S. senators, Adam Schiff and Alex Padilla, have declined to take a position on the wealth tax. So, too, another well-known California Democrat, former Vice President Kamala Harris.

Perhaps the biggest source of intrigue, though, is a non-Californian. Representative Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez of New York, who once wore a “tax the rich” gown to the Met Gala and who has been traveling the country with Sanders on what they’ve named the Fighting Oligarchy Tour. In August, Regan publicly encouraged her to get behind Proposition 40, but she has yet to do so.

One reason might be that organized labor as well as progressive advocacy groups are divided over the measure. While many of them support Proposition 40, it has also drawn notable opposition. Planned Parenthood Affiliates of California and the N. A. A. C. P. California Hawaii are both against it. So, too, is the California Teachers Association. The C. T. A., which has around 300,000 members, is sponsoring a different ballot initiative, Proposition 3, that would extend current California income tax rates for high earners in order to fund public education and community colleges. Its president, David Goldberg, says that his organization wanted to prioritize Proposition 3.

But he acknowledges that the C. T. A. also has misgivings about Proposition 40. One issue is that it earmarks almost nothing for education. Another is that it won’t generate sustained revenue. Goldberg says that waging a bloody fight over a one-time tax isn’t worth the political capital. “We’re taking on the wealthiest people in the history of the world,” he says, “and we have to be tactically really smart.”

Goldberg went on to tell me that Regan hadn’t helped his cause. According to Goldberg, Regan met with two of his colleagues via Zoom in September in the hope of persuading the C. T. A. to back Proposition 40. “It was a very disrespectful meeting in a lot of ways, frankly,” Goldberg says. “He ended up just hanging up on them.” (Regan’s spokeswoman says he wasn’t angry; the two sides simply disagreed, and the meeting ended.)

Regan’s personal conduct has become an issue in the campaign. Last month, several California news organizations reported that Regan has been accused of using threats and coercion to try to force other labor leaders to support Proposition 40. They also cited allegations that he had a history of resorting to verbal and physical intimidation. Regan denies these claims and says they are a smear campaign orchestrated by his billionaire opponents.

Billionaires aren’t his only detractors at the moment, however. Katie Porter, a former California congresswoman who earned a reputation as a progressive stalwart during her six years in Washington, told me that Proposition 40 was “another ill-conceived, rushed Dave Regan special.” She, too, was troubled by the one-time nature of the tax and the outsize allocation of money to health care and suggested that it was little more than a gimmicky response to profoundly serious public policy issues. “I am a certified Proposition 40 hater,” Porter told me. “It plays on — in a very facile and dangerous way — righteous political anger and deep economic anxiety.”

In her view, it was a lose-lose proposition. If the ballot measure is defeated, it will convince many Americans that the power of billionaires is unassailable, which will make it harder to muster support for more ambitious efforts to tackle inequality. And conversely, if it passes and proves to be a bust for one reason or another, which Porter fears will be the case, it will be a heavy blow for the progressive movement. If a billionaire tax enacted in a state that is “home to one of every six Americans” were to fail, she said, it would “tremendously set back the cause of progressive solutions for our economy. It will be devastating.”

Recent history offers arguably the most persuasive case against Proposition 40. In 1990, a dozen European countries had wealth taxes. Now, only three do. Saez and Zucman’s native France had a particularly long and vexed experiment with a wealth tax before finally jettisoning it. First introduced in 1981, it came to be known as the I. S. F., which stood for impôt de solidarité sur les fortunes, or the solidarity tax on wealth. But a popular quip was that the initials stood for invitation à sortir de France, or the invitation to leave France, and in the more than three decades that the tax was in effect, many French citizens did leave, at some cost to the economy. It was eliminated in 2017.

Saez and Zucman have closely studied France’s failed wealth tax. As they see it, one major design flaw was that its threshold was too low: It applied to households with a minimum net worth equivalent to about 1 million euros. But that pulled in a lot of people whose wealth was tied up in their homes or apartments, and the tax was a hardship for many of them. At the same time, it was riddled with loopholes that allowed the superrich to avoid paying it. Anyone who owned 25 percent or more of a business, for instance, was not subject to the tax. “The French experience with wealth taxation is precisely what you don’t want to do,” Saez told me.

He and other proponents of the California billionaire tax are confident that it will succeed where others failed because it contains no exemptions, applies to only a small number of people and will be difficult if not impossible to evade. Brian Galle, the Berkeley law professor who helped write the tax, says that because it will be retroactively effective to Jan. 1 of this year, any billionaire whose primary residence was in California at that time would have to pay.

Even someone who slipped away just ahead of that date will have a hard time dodging the tax, according to Galle, who cites Brin as an example. To avoid paying, Brin would have to convince the California Franchise Tax Board that his life was truly anchored in Nevada when the tax was introduced. But given that he acquired the Tahoe property only in December, that might be tough to prove. “Spending time in your fifth vacation home does not a Nevada resident make,” Galle says. (Brin did not respond to requests for comment.)

As part of the drafting process, Galle and his colleagues consulted with the F. T. B. and came away confident that it would be able to enforce the tax efficiently and comprehensively. One reason for their confidence: Around 65 percent of the total wealth of California’s billionaires is held in publicly traded stocks. For the four wealthiest — Brin, Page, Huang and Mark Zuckerberg, who now have a combined net worth of nearly $1 trillion — it approaches 100 percent. Tallying up the fortunes of extraordinarily rich people can be difficult because they often own things that are hard to value precisely, such as rare artwork, and also because their businesses and side ventures are sometimes structured in opaque ways. The fact, though, that most of the wealth of California’s billionaires is tied up in publicly traded assets should make the job of the tax board easier. It would presumably also make it easier to withstand challenges from billionaires disputing their tax assessments.

If Proposition 40 passes, it will undoubtedly be challenged on constitutional grounds. Two years ago, in Moore v. United States, the Supreme Court indicated that it would strike down a federal wealth tax if one were enacted. But Galle notes that in a separate opinion, Justice Clarence Thomas said that federal restrictions on taxing unrealized gains didn’t apply at the state level, which seemed to suggest that he, at least, was open to the idea of state-administered wealth taxes like the one California is considering. Galle was heartened by Thomas’s reading of the laws but is taking nothing for granted: While he doesn’t think there is a plausible case to be made that Proposition 40 is unconstitutional, he says the current Supreme Court is nothing if not unpredictable. “If you had any court other than this one, I’d have 99.9 percent certainty,” he says.

But before it can reach the Supreme Court, the tax has to be approved by voters, and the outcome is far from certain. On the same day that he held the town hall meeting, Khanna joined Regan for a Proposition 40 rally at a union office in San Jose. Khanna lauded Regan as “a national hero” for getting the measure on the ballot and said that if it prevailed in November, it would be “a shot heard around the nation and the world.” But he conceded that it was going to be a tough battle. “We would win in a landslide if it weren’t for one thing,” Khanna said. “Well, guess what the one thing is? The money.”

The money seems to be having an impact. In March, The Los Angeles Times commissioned a poll by the Institute of Governmental Studies at U. C.-Berkeley that found overwhelming support for the wealth tax: Of the 5,019 registered voters who were surveyed, 52 percent favored it, with just 33 percent opposed. But when a similar poll was conducted in early August, the gap had narrowed considerably, with 48 percent supporting the tax and 41 percent against it. By mid-September, the margin had shrunk even more, to 45 percent in favor and 42 percent opposed.

Regan told me that he expected opponents of Proposition 40 to burn through at least $400 million. By contrast, his union had raised just $32 million. But he also suggested that the best advertisement for Proposition 40 was the billionaires themselves. Their aggrieved response to the proposed tax, and the vast sums of money they were spending to defeat the ballot initiative, was likely, he argued, to make people even more inclined to vote for it. “Keep talking, Sergey,” Regan said. “Keep talking, Garry Tan. Keep talking, Peter Thiel, because you guys are not the best messengers.”

Michael Steinberger is a contributing writer for the magazine and the author of “The Philosopher in the Valley: Alex Karp, Palantir and the Rise of the Surveillance State.”

Social Security Says Retirees Will Get a 3.6% Raise for 2027

The New York Times October 14, 2026

Advertisement

Supported by

The cost of living adjustment, or COLA, for next year will be the largest since 2023. Retirees will see the extra money in their benefit checks starting in January.

By Mark Miller

Retirees and other beneficiaries will receive the largest annual cost-of-living adjustment since 2023 in their Social Security checks next year, driven mainly by persistently high inflation.

The Social Security Administration announced a 3.6 percent annual cost-of-living adjustment, or COLA, on Wednesday after the release of government inflation data for September. The COLA is determined using the Consumer Price Index for Urban Wage Earners and Clerical Workers, or CPI-W. The index values for July, August and September are averaged to produce the official adjustment figure.

For the average Social Security beneficiary, the COLA will translate to a $75 monthly increase, the government said. However, the actual amount for any retiree enrolled in Medicare will vary. The Part B premium, which covers outpatient care, is usually deducted from Social Security benefits, changing net COLAs. Separately, Social Security said the maximum amount of earnings subject to the Social Security payroll tax would rise to $192,300, up from $184,500 this year.

The Medicare board of trustees has projected that the monthly Part B premium will rise $6.60 in 2027 to $209.50. The final figure will be released this fall, but if the projection holds, most beneficiaries will receive net COLAs of at least 3 percent.

Other Medicare costs are rising, too. Monthly premiums for some prescription drug plans, which are known as Part D, will jump $50 or more next year — although plenty of lower-cost options are available for people who shop the market during the annual enrollment period that begins Thursday. In addition, costs will rise and supplemental benefits will be reduced in private Medicare Advantage plans next year.

The CPI-W reflects price changes for goods and services that working people buy and use, and it is under-weighted for health care costs, which have an outsize impact on older Americans. The health spending burden for people enrolled in Medicare is more than twice as large as that of other households, accounting for 14 percent of total spending in 2024, according to KFF, a health care research nonprofit.

The COLA announcement comes at a time when Americans are feeling less confident that they will have enough money to live comfortably in retirement. An annual survey released this year by the Employee Benefit Research Institute found that 61 percent of workers were confident they will have enough money to be comfortable in retirement, down from 72 percent in 2021; retiree confidence fell to 73 percent from 80 percent during the same period.

“For retirees who aren’t confident, inflation and the rising cost of living are overwhelmingly the top reason,” said Craig Copeland, director of wealth benefits research at E. B. R. I.

The automatic COLA is one of Social Security’s most valuable features, but some advocates have long argued that it isn’t adequate to help seniors cope with inflation — and that the CPI-W should be replaced by a yardstick that does a better job measuring the inflation that affects retirees.

A new AARP survey found that just 23 percent of adults over age 50 think a 3.5 percent COLA keeps up with rising prices. “For most people, the COLA just isn’t enough to keep up with what they see as rising costs in their life,” said Bill Sweeney, AARP’s senior vice president for government affairs. “When they go to the grocery store or fill up their tank with gas or get a notice that their rent is going up, it’s almost always higher than those numbers.”

Worries about the future of Social Security itself also are eroding confidence.

Social Security’s trustees projected in June that the retirement trust fund would be exhausted in 2032 — just six years from now. The combined retirement and disability funds are projected to run dry in 2034. Those are likely best-case projections, as key underlying factors have deteriorated over the past year, including lower long-range assumptions about the size of the U. S. population, which were affected by lower projections on the number of children women will have.

Trust fund exhaustion does not mean Social Security would stop paying benefits altogether, but it would set off an across-the-board cut of about 22 percent for retirement benefits in 2032, absent congressional action, according to the Social Security trustees. The top concern for retirees in this year’s E. B. R. I. survey was possible government changes.

“People are realizing that they’re going to have to make adjustments in their expectations of what the benefits are going to be in the future,” Mr. Copeland said.

The AARP survey found that 46 percent would cut back on dining out if benefits were reduced 5 percent — but a nearly equal share, 45 percent, said they would have to cut back on groceries and basic household items.

Retirement savings and investments can help plug the gap in meeting living costs, but those savings are available mainly to higher-income people. Among households ages 55 to 64 that have a 401(k) account, the median balance for the lowest fifth in 2022 was just $25,000 — and $220,000 in the third fifth, according to the Center for Retirement Research at Boston College.

At the same time, poverty rates for the lowest-income households are rising. The Census Bureau reported recently that the rate of poverty for Americans 65 and older rose to 15.4 percent in 2025 — the highest rate for all age groups, and up sharply from 12 percent in 2019.

The Social Security Administration will notify recipients about their new benefit amounts by mail and online starting in early December, and the COLA will be added in January to the benefits of millions of Americans, a group that includes retirees and their spouses and survivors, as well as those who receive disability benefits and Supplemental Security Income.

Some older people also will receive a separate one-time payment of $90 this month from the government. Characterized by the Trump administration as a Part B “premium rebate,” the payment will be sent by direct deposit or check to 21 million people enrolled in Part B under traditional fee-for-service Medicare. The payments, before the midterm elections, were described by the White House as a “historic action to help senior citizens,” but KFF noted that the 2027 projected Part B premium increase alone would consume most of the $90 — and that less than half of all beneficiaries will get it, since Medicare Advantage enrollees are ineligible.

“An extra $90 in the bank won’t hurt,” said Juliette Cubanski, vice president and director of the program on Medicare policy at KFF. “But it’s not likely to stretch very far to cover higher costs for everyday essentials like putting food on the table, filling up the gas tank and paying the utility bill.”

A Trump Backlash Endangers Susan Collins, the Only Blue-State G. O. P. Senator

The New York Times October 14, 2026

The race in Maine, which could decide Senate control, is testing whether there is still room for Ms. Collins’s brand of moderation during a sharply polarizing presidency.

Connections Coach

The race in Maine, which could decide Senate control, is testing whether there is still room for Ms. Collins’s brand of moderation during a sharply polarizing presidency.

Senator Susan Collins of Maine has cultivated a reputation as an independent-minded Republican who delivers for her state. As she seeks re-election, she faces a growing set of political challenges. Credit... Kenny Holston/The New York Times

Supported by

By Tim Balk

Reporting from Fryeburg, Maine

Senator Susan Collins, Republican of Maine, is facing a moment like no other in her three decades in office, one that is testing whether her moderate brand of politics can survive perhaps the most polarizing presidency in modern history.

Ms. Collins, a symbol of centrism and a paragon of decorum, has won five elections in a state that reliably votes for Democrats in presidential races by burnishing her reputation as an independent-minded Republican who delivers for Maine. But this year, she is battling forces that are squeezing her from several directions: a working-class rage about rising prices, a call for generational change and, above all, a burning desire among many voters to make a statement against President Trump’s divisive second term.

“I respect her,” Liz McCormack, 65, a Democrat and retired professor in the coastal college town of Brunswick, said one recent day as she cast a ballot for Ms. Collins’s Democratic challenger, Troy Jackson. Ms. McCormack voted for Ms. Collins in 2020, she said. But this time, she added, “it’s important to send a message.”

Ms. Collins’s predicament, as well as her enduring strength, was on display last week as she weaved through a crowded, sprawling fair in Fryeburg, Maine.

There were well-wishers who effusively thanked her for service. And children who flocked to her side. But then there was the emotional farmer, undecided in the Senate race, who stopped her to tell her how hard it was to make ends meet. And the young man who jeered her as she argued that Maine voters really were not all that hungry for change.

“There are always a few exceptions,” Ms. Collins said, smiling, as the heckler shouted at her that Mainers couldn’t “eat road salt,” a line Mr. Jackson had delivered in a debate in an effort to dismiss her work to exempt road salt from Mr. Trump’s tariffs.

Ms. Collins, 73, a skeptic of the president who is among the last of a nearly extinct breed of congressional Republicans, is in the fight of her political life, trying to win in a blue state where many voters are incensed with Mr. Trump and say that she has not opposed him strongly enough. With the Nov. 3 election approaching, the senator has been playing political Whac-a-Mole, pleading with administration officials to address Trump-inflicted challenges while straining to persuade voters that she is a check on his presidency.

Her race against Mr. Jackson — a progressive Democrat, logger and former state lawmaker who brought up Mr. Trump more than 15 times in their first debate — could determine control of the Senate during Mr. Trump’s last two years in office. Polls show a tight race.

Ms. Collins has managed to survive as a blue-state Republican in the Trump era by selectively breaking with the president and maintaining her image as a bridge builder. The last time she was on the ballot, in 2020, she triumphed by nine percentage points even as Mr. Trump fell in Maine by the same margin. In Maine, a state where about a third of the voters are unaffiliated, a Democratic-leaning share of the electorate has long stood by Ms. Collins, expressing appreciation for her independence, legislative acumen and work to secure resources for Maine as chair of the powerful Appropriations Committee.

But it’s not clear how many of those voters will stick by her this time around.

Some who supported the senator in the past say they remain furious, years later, over Ms. Collins’s connection to the overturning of Roe v. Wade. In 2018, Ms. Collins cast one of the deciding votes to confirm a Supreme Court justice, Brett M. Kavanaugh, who joined the Supreme Court’s 2022 decision erasing a federal abortion right. Ms. Collins, who supports abortion rights, has stood by her vote for Mr. Kavanaugh as it has become a central issue in the election.

Democratic-leaning voters who have supported her in the past were split as they cast ballots one recent morning in Lewiston, a working-class former mill town on the Androscoggin River that Ms. Collins carried in 2020 even as Mr. Trump lost it by 14 points.

Pauline Castonguay, a 78-year-old Democrat, said she was sticking with Ms. Collins.

“She’s done a good job,” Ms. Castonguay said. “I don’t always agree with her. But I think she does vote according to her conscience. I think she’s been a very dedicated employee.”

But Roger Caron, 78, a retired barber and an independent who backed Ms. Collins in the past, said that “this cycle” it was time for the senator to go.

“I’m not voting for any Republicans until Trump is out of office,” Mr. Caron said, expressing disgust with the president’s second term.

In recent months, Ms. Collins has sought to resolve challenge after challenge posed by the president.

Faced with surging heating oil prices because of the war in Iran, Ms. Collins pushed the administration to expedite the release of federal heating assistance for lower-income Maine residents. (The president earlier this year sought to end the heating assistance program.)

Faced with a public outcry over the killing of an immigrant by an Immigration and Customs Enforcement agent in Maine in July, Ms. Collins said she had secured funding for body-worn cameras, de-escalation training for ICE officers and investigations into misconduct. (Before the killing, she had taken credit for pushing ICE agents to pull out of Maine in response to an earlier public outcry, only to watch the issue return.)

And faced with Mr. Trump’s suggestion that he was likely to visit Maine to campaign, Ms. Collins has sought to rule out the notion.

“I’m not exactly his favorite senator,” Ms. Collins said at one debate.

But some in Maine say that is not enough. They argue that Ms. Collins has voted with Mr. Trump when it matters, and that she has not done enough to curb his immigration crackdown, tariffs policies or the war in Iran, which she initially supported but has turned against.

“She has in my view been a disappointment in the past few years,” said Phyllis Blackstone, 77, of Brunswick, a Democrat and self-described former Collins voter casting a ballot for Mr. Jackson this year. “When it comes time to vote, she does as Trump would like her to do.”

A New York Times analysis of Ms. Collins’s voting record found that she voted against Mr. Trump’s policies more often than anyone else in her party, but that she seldom changed an outcome.

Ms. Collins has never voted for Mr. Trump, according to her campaign, and is one of just a few remaining Republican senators who voted to convict Mr. Trump of inciting an insurrection in his impeachment trial in 2021.

Now many voters in Maine say they’re desperate to respond at the ballot box to the Trump administration. And in contrast with 2020, when Ms. Collins won, this time those looking to vent frustration at Mr. Trump don’t have the option of splitting their tickets.

At Bowdoin College in Brunswick, students lounging under a colorful canopy of changing leaves said the Trump administration and Maine’s role in the battle for Senate control made Ms. Collins’s race feel like one of grave importance.

Rosa K. Slack, 19, a sophomore and a lifelong Maine resident, said she might be open to voting for Republicans in the future but was eager to vote against Ms. Collins, because she felt it would mean removing an ally of Mr. Trump.

“I don’t like Trump,” Ms. Slack said, “so I don’t like that she’s willing to associate herself with that as a Mainer.”

Back in Lewiston, the working-class city where Mr. Trump was trounced and Ms. Collins prevailed in 2020, the statewide tug of war over Ms. Collins’s future was playing out in miniature as Carol and Dan Quinn, two 81-year-old independents, picked up absentee ballots.

Ms. Quinn, a retired teacher, said she was planning to vote against Ms. Collins, declaring that the senator was “under Trump’s thumb.” Mr. Quinn, however, said he expected to vote for Ms. Collins — and for Democrats on the rest of his Maine ballot.

“Generally I’d like to see the Democrats win,” Mr. Quinn, a retired businessman, said of the battle for Senate control. But he added that he was unimpressed by Mr. Jackson and still appreciative of the senator. “Susan Collins has done a lot for this state.”

Ms. Quinn jawed back.

“She’s too old,” she said. “I don’t trust her.”

Mr. Quinn replied that he disagreed.

Ms. Quinn chuckled, vowing to try to persuade her husband before he cast his ballot.

“We’re not,” she said, “going to be getting along the rest of the day.”
